# Character State

Updated after Batch 0002 (Volume 01, Chapters 11–20). Read the thread and condition entries below against `state/continuity.md`, which is the authority. Ages are canon; states are as of the close of Chapter 20 / the top of Tuesday 21 October 2024.

## Elias Venn, 32

**Where he is now.** Suspended from night duty since 07:00 on Sunday 11 October. Permitted: building keys, current card, the service lane, the loading-bay desk, the security room, the ground floor, staff facilities, and attendance at a re-inspection *as escort, ground floor only, not in the room*. Barred: the Hall of Former Faiths, the Gallery of Unnamed Hands, the conservation floor, the Quiet Wing. In the payroll system the restriction is **scheduled leave in lieu of notice** — unpaid and un-dismissible — and Standing Instruction 14 clause 7 forbids any reference to fault in any record about him, and refers any further incident of the same cause to a hearing. He is therefore in a state no institution in the book knows how to describe.

**What he wants, on the page.** Not the post. The review on the fourth Monday is a thing he has stopped planning for. What he wants on the page now is **to stop being the only place a fact lives**, and he got that in Chapter 18 and it cost him the only thing he had, and he is not sure yet that it was a fair trade.

**What he has.** A pocket notebook. An unspent name. A police form with a crime number on it. A headcount in the notebook against *a number* in a shared log. A sealed envelope in a Board Secretary's file that is now, by clause six, inside the building. A longhand copy of a 1974 carbon receipt, which is the only evidence in the world that a bundle of wage sheets came into this building in a van with a lamp. Nine objects and forty-one drawers. A blue disc with a consignor's initials on it. Two hundred and ten dead letters behind him and a booking book in front of him that he had never read in two years.

**Skill state. Stage 1 — Echo, confirmed, and it is a narrow confirmation.** He can make deliberate contact within a handspan of a saint-bearing object while focusing on one bounded question, and he gets the object's **last meaningful act**, and he cannot separate the act from the point of view it was seen from. On Monday 12 October he asked a lamp who carried it out of the last building it was in and got a kitchen. He can feel a resonant room and he cannot read a god. He is **not** able to do it on demand, he is not able to ask a question of a room, and he has no procedure at all for the thing the object does that matters most, which is reach an unconsenting person.

**The flaw, on the page, and it has stopped being a habit and started being a method.** Chapter 11: he says the sentence about the letter out loud once so that it is a thing that has been said, and does not take the envelope. Chapter 13: he hands over four photographs and four hundred words of self-indictment, and the institution puts them back in a file the next Monday. Chapter 15: he stands at a basement door with his hand on it and does not open it, and goes upstairs and tells Mara everything instead — *the same thing, the second time, and this time because it is the only other option he has left.* Chapter 16: **he says the true count's location, date, row, plinth and case in a street in front of a glass door with eleven people behind it, and he had four seconds in which to say *I can't tell you that*, and it was true, and he did not take it.** Chapter 17: he trespasses on a cleared site alone because he is frightened of what he will find out, and a man of seventy-four goes into a canal. Chapter 18: he says the word *lying* out loud, in a room with a card cutter in it, to a woman who has earned the right to say the word first. Chapter 20: he stands on a landing and does not say one word to a woman walking past, because Mara told him not to, and it cost him more than the canal.

**Costs unpaid and still owed.** A promise to tell Mara everything, broken twice in Chapter 10 and now broken a second time in Chapter 16 and *named* in Chapter 18. The knowledge that the only person who can build a form for a night cleaner has told him he will not write a word of it. A police number with his name on it. The bin of blue bales that went out of the door on Friday morning with eleven years of a dead man's post in it, and three people who knew, and a book whose head nobody reads. And the number: he knows what ninety-four counts, and he knows the pay run listed fewer than were on the gate, and he has not told Mara, and he is aware on Monday the nineteenth of November that he will have to decide.

**What he is frightened of, specifically.** Not the building and not the number any more. **A hundred people in this city who need him to do it, and nobody to say the word.** A seventy-eight-year-old pay clerk in a chair by a lit gas fire said it to him before he said it to himself, and she is the only person in the volume who has said anything he did not already know.

## Mara Iles, 28

**Where she is now.** In the conservation office on the first floor of a building she has been in for five years, with a shared protocol log whose result field says *a number*, a box under it that is blank, a covering note of Sunday 18 October to the Board Secretary and the audit provider, a written request to the Board for authority to open a leaseholder's locker, a four-hundred-word statement she did not write and has read, and a form she is building for a man in overalls that he will not be allowed near.

**Her position on the two Chapter 9 conditions.** The first is intact to the letter and defeated him anyway. The second he broke by silence and then by lying to her face. **In Chapter 18 she says what she is doing about it and it is not forgiveness:** everything goes in the file the same day with a number on it, and if the file cannot take it she will build a form that can, and **nobody in this building decides on their own that a person is safer not knowing** — and then, at the stair door, the thing that is the relationship: *we are not us. There is a protocol and there are two conditions and one of them is a relationship, and the relationship is that there is no more private channel, and I am telling you that so that you understand that you are not getting it back.*

**What she has done that nobody else has done.** Clause seven of Standing Instruction 14, in the margin, initialled and dated Sunday afternoon: *the Museum will not destroy any record of an individual employee held by the municipal payroll bureau without first obtaining the bureau's own account in writing.* A permanent institutional instrument, drafted in about forty minutes on a Sunday, by a person who does not sleep on Sundays. And the Annexe C re-certification of the Hall in one sentence, which she calls *a lie of omission of a size I have signed about four hundred times.*

**What she has refused, out loud and for stated reasons.** To ring Hattie Bramm. To let him ask Hattie Bramm. To write a name in the account box on the shared protocol form. To let him write a word of the form she is building for Perry Loach. And to accept, in the note of 18 October, that a name in a document is a document — she has put **Wendel Case** in a covering note to a member of the Board and nowhere else and she says so out loud, and she knows exactly what she has done.

**Her fault, if there is one, in this batch.** She read the day book every day from 7 October and told him so on Tuesday, which is two days of silence she chose, and she is clear-eyed about it and has not forgiven herself for it and has not forgiven him either.

**Wants from him.** The result in the shared log in her hand exactly as it comes. And everything, every time. He has now broken the second one twice — once by omission, once by a lie, once knowingly and publicly — and she has said in writing what she will do about it, and she is going to do it.

**Owes nothing and has not been thanked.** She has still never had the reading. He has still not asked her for it.

## Ivo Pell, 25

**Where he is now.** On systems days 07:00–15:00, Tuesday to Saturday, and on junior-guard nights, and he has spent Batch 0002 not being asked for anything by the man he works with, and not being protected from anything either.

**What he has done, and it is the batch's most consequential act of institutional repair.** He ran an eight-week systems inventory nobody asked him to run, and in it he restored the **schedule of collections** from the paper series against the Registry's register, and in the third section of a document about bin numbers he wrote a schedule note with a number in it: *loading bay, cage 2, current position blocks the swing of a door on a designated route of escape.* Nobody had to be brave about a fire thing. They moved a cage. And he has the Registry's weekly job list, a rota of four assessors, and a material-change slot on 19 November that was empty on Monday and is not empty now, and he booked it without being asked and told Elias about it afterwards, in a workshop doorway, and said: *if you had asked me, I would have done it in a fortnight and it would have been worse.*

**His exposure, which has grown without his noticing.** He is on the fault report of 10 October, a co-defendant by silence, and he is now also the man who is dealing with the Registrar's twenty-two letters and the Registry's rota and the Museum's schedule of collections, and he has a copy of a bulletin nobody else has. He has also, without saying so, become the person who can get Elias into rooms he is barred from. He has not noticed. He has a sister with a chest and no lungs to speak of and a Saturday systems day and a night before it.

**Wants.** To be a certified Civic Works engineer, to be useful, and to go home. He has begun, deliberately, to be the reason a man can take risks at all, and he has not yet noticed that this is the dependency the series needs him to outgrow.

## Dael Ordway, 44

**Where he is now.** Off nights since 11 October, not the supervisor this week, and came onto the floor on Tuesday 20 October afternoon to do the thing the whole volume turns on. **He did not put the tour on the calendar and he does not own it.** Not the press, not the Board, not the Director: **Dael read the green booking book on the security desk that he had never read in eleven years, found a school group of thirty-eight booked in September, and worked out that a public event defers the disposition under a clause in the disposition's own paperwork — **not** under any of the four conditions Anja Ward issued on 15 October, which contain no such clause — and moved the case forward three weeks by waking thirty-eight children up. He is not a man who books a school group; he is a man who reads one.**

**His speech in Chapter 20 is the chapter's best argument and it is his own reasoning.** *Annexe D is the best document this building has produced in my lifetime and it will hold that bell in that case for ever, and it will do nothing whatever for sixty-one people, because a classification is not a wage.* *I am going to be the man who wakes thirty-eight children up on a Friday morning in November, and I have never done anything else in eleven years that frightened me as much, and I am doing it, and I am not doing it for you and I am not doing it for the mill and I am not doing it for that woman on the stair landing. I am doing it because it is the only card on the table.*

**He is not clean and he is not a villain.** He cannot sign the docket any more, because the standing instruction says a director's signature is not sufficient, and he knew that before anybody told him. He has been signing a false cause for a fortnight and has told nobody but the man he did it to. He refused Elias a telephone number because a member of the public's address in a Museum file is a thing this institution has just been told it is bad at. And he has read the Act on limitation, in the evenings, and says so, and calls it ridiculous.

**Knows.** Everything in the systems. The 1974 receipt's existence, in outline. The records clerk's post.

**Does not know.** The twenty minutes, the corridor, the device book before Sunday, the reading, the lamp, Doreen Sarn, the second envelope. He is going to find all of it out in Movement 4 and he is going to write the same four lines about it.

## Anja Ward, 53

**Where she is now.** Issuing standing conditions in a card the size of a small envelope, telephoning Northglass, and putting four clauses on Standing Instruction 14 that are all aimed at this building and not one of them at a suspended night guard, and saying out loud that this is not generosity. She has been instructed as a director by two solicitors to do a thing she cannot tell Elias about and will not be able to say publicly that she did, because the privilege is hers and not his and he did not make it hers.

**What she has given away by accident.** Clause two, requiring a countersignature from a person who is not an officer or employee, is the only thing standing between a bell and a van, and she says so, and she also says that it is standing there by accident. She has signed nothing. She has written a minute. She cannot be got.

**Her cover-up** is not on the page and must not be. The missing signatory on the Quiet Wing instruction is the same eighteen years as the Casket, and nobody in this batch has noticed.

## Doreen Sarn, 78

**On the page as of Wednesday 14 October and fully realised by Sunday 18.** 12 Ferry Bank, second floor, first door, a cardigan over a pinafore, a net curtain, a plastic tumbler on the sill, a lamp that is the mate of the Museum's in a press for thirty-one years, a blue plastic folder, nine years of letters with a crest on them, and a hardback cash book with a rubber band round it which she has never surrendered to Elias, to Mara, to Quill & Frayle, to the Registry, or to anybody, and is not going to.

**What she is.** Sillick Mill's pay clerk. She bought a lamp off the forecourt on the last morning for two shillings. She put the pay run's figure onto the wage sheet on the last morning because a mill has to close on a Friday, and she has known she did it for thirty-one years, and she told a stranger in her front room before he could get it out of her. She is the volume's moral centre and she is not a device and she is not an oracle and she has not forgiven anybody.

**What she said that the whole volume is built on.** *A book gets lent. A book gets copied. A book gets read in a room with the people it is about in it, and then it belongs to more than one person and nobody can be got at over it. And the thing you've got is a coat pocket, and a coat pocket is a book with one reader, and one reader is a liability.* And: *you'd better not be the only one who can do it. Because if you're the only one, then by Christmas there's a hundred people in this city who need you to do it, and you will do it, because you can't not, and you will be twenty-six years old in a bad night with a headache and a hundred people waiting, and there'll be nobody to say the word.*

**What she has given.** The second envelope, dated 22 October 1974, and the reason the mill's post came back to the mill, and therefore that item 5 on the 1974 receipt is very probably her own envelope. And, on Sunday, the whole of the reading, told to him and not by him, including the phrase.

**What she will not do.** She will not surrender the cash book. She will not read it to a lawyer, a trustee, a Board or a museum, and her reason is the volume's reason: *a copy in a solicitor's drawer is a copy in a drawer. I want it read in a room with the people who are affected in it.*

## Hattie Bramm, 54

**On the page as of Friday 16 October and fully realised by Tuesday 20.** Gate clerk on the closing shift; the woman who wrote the figures down when a foreman called them; one of the sixty-one; one of the ninety-four; nine years of letters; a shortfall list; a file with a stranger's name written in the back of it; **and a plastic wallet in her coat with an unopened letter in it from the site's new owner offering an independent review of the final shortfall at the company's cost, without admission of liability, on the basis that her correspondence ceases for twelve months.** She has not answered it and told nobody, and she showed it to Elias on a wet pavement because he is the only person in the city who has been rude to her for money.

**Her function, and it is the batch's moral engine.** She is the one who understands that a priced claim becomes a negotiated claim, and that the ledger is the only thing that cannot be divided up and shared out. She is the one who makes Elias say *I have never heard of it* and look at her while he says it. She is the one who told him *don't come to my queue and ask me to be careful with a number* and then, four hours later, was the reason anything came of anything. And she is the one who watched him walk past a man in a chair and said nothing, which she will use on him eventually and has not yet.

**What she now knows.** That the Museum is holding the object, that the box has gone out to a solicitor's store in Northglass, that there is a hearing on the twenty-eighth, that an assessor has looked at the bell and written *no devotional content* on a sheet, and that the man who sat in her chair in September works in the building that has the paper.

**What she still does not know.** That he can read objects. He has never told her and there is no version of Chapter 20 in which he is going to.

## Perry Loach, 61 (prose: *a night cleaner in his early sixties*)

**Still the only person the object has been shown to reach, and still in no record of any kind — and Batch 0002 has made that worse, deliberately, by making the paperwork better.** The cleaners are not on the municipal payroll bureau. They are on a services contract, in a printed grid headed **CLEANING — 0**, with hours split by room and **no column in which a person can be written**. The time book and punch cards that recorded him are abolished on 1 November. The only form in the city that would take his own words is blank, and the woman who would build it has said she will, and will not let Elias near it.

**He was in Hattie Bramm's queue on Friday 16 October** — a man in his sixties in a plain dark coat with a shopping bag, a tin of tea in it, work boots resoled once, a hearing aid he does not turn on, and a piece of paper with nothing on it — for an hour, and he did not speak to anybody, and Elias stood three feet from him and walked out.

**The numeral never appears in the same chapter as his name, and never in the same paragraph as the short-paid count.** Chapter 15 names him and has Case's age in the same letter; the two are separated by more than a hundred lines and are not of the same kind.

## Aurelia Roth, 34 — Registry assessor (introduced Ch 20)

Northglass. Six years in. The person who wrote the *Schedule 12* classification on 12 October, issued **Annexe D** — a **permanent** classification, *confidence: medium*, *the object is to be displayed, under no circumstances is it to be removed from public view by a museum acting alone* — and who re-did the whole material examination on 20 October on the strength of **nothing the case has produced**, and told him so, and told him that the request form is a statutory mechanism that has been on the notice board for six years and has been used four times in that period, three of them by people asking for a painting re-hung. **She is the reason the bell is in a public room for the rest of the volume and she is not a friend of anybody in it.** She is a duty with a satchel and it happened to work.

## Minor working people, established and added in Batch 0002

- **Fenn** — label-room finisher, overnight. **She said no twice and both noes were correct, and she corrected his count of the drawer, and she read the drawer at two o'clock in the afternoon on the Tuesday and squared the stack and took his signal off it.** Unchanged as a person; no longer a mystery.
- **Priest** — Museum duty manager, nineteen years in post. Witnessed the day-book entries, carried the sealed envelope, signed the escort column for Mara, told Hattie Bramm that her file said she is a Museum visitor, and says the plain version. **She is on the Registry rota in Ivo's list of people dealing with the Museum this week.** Unchanged.
- **Spence** — Museum metals conservator, the one who opened the case on 12 October and re-anchored it on 20. Surname only, as Fenn is.
- **Ottilie Grange** — Registry assessor, Northglass, of 12 October. The *Schedule 12* classification, the *not observed* note, the four photographs with a scale rule, the brass anchor plates, the QA/1187 tag, the **duly exported** flag, and the retiring third ward-bell in QA/N/34. **A bureaucrat whose job is notices, not answers, and who stopped at the word *partial*.**
- **Nunn** — transfer clerk, Hallowgate Foyston, the man with the cage and the docket and the witness line that does not ask for the man's own words. Not a villain. Corrected a location at Elias's instruction and initialled it in the margin.
- **Cheryl Vosper** — claims manager, Vail & Thwaite, not a lawyer, has a drawer. Told a suspended night guard to his face that his supervisor's summary is fine; kept his 400-word statement and dated it in ninety seconds because it came to the policy address and not to the building; asked him not to be in the hearing on 28 October; and rang on Tuesday to ask whether the Museum has a record of an item of post in the name of **W. Case**, and was told, completely straight, that he is not permitted to look and that there is a form at the desk anybody can use.
- **Cliff Sowerby, 74, of 14 Sillick Lane** — Sillick Mill dye-house fitter's mate's son, heating engineer retired, holder of the last key to a steel box on a shelf behind the site-office meter. **In hospital from Saturday 17 October with a dislocated shoulder, a fractured wrist, an impact to the head, and a temperature of about thirty-four, after going through a staging board on a flooded slipway that had no handrail because the handrail was off and a scaffold board had been thrown across.** The one time his name is on the page in this volume, it is Doreen Sarn saying it, and she is saying it about a lamp on a shoulder, and he is not a Case and must not drift into being one.
- **A name on a leaving card** — *MALLOY, J. — Grade 2*, 2,041 hours and *nil*. Furniture, and the only joke in the batch.

## Relationships, end of batch

- **Elias / Mara:** adversarial professional respect that has now been broken twice and repaired with a protocol. **No intimacy, no romance.** The slow burn has started with a condition and the condition *is* the relationship: there is no more private channel, and she has told him he is not getting it back, and she is building a form for a night cleaner that he will not be allowed to write a word of, and she is doing it for the man who went into a canal for him and not for him.
- **Elias / Ivo:** working friendship that has in this batch become *an unasked favour, twice, and a mutual understanding about what that means.* Ivo has named the flaw and then, in Chapter 17, discovered that Elias was trespassing alone while he was doing a systems day, and has not yet said so to him. **That conversation is owed and is not owed to this batch.**
- **Elias / Dael:** deferred-to became managed became **deferred-to again, by choice, with reasons Dael can articulate.** Dael refused him a telephone number and told him why, and told him the records clerk's post is gone, and asked him for one favour and got it.
- **Elias / Hattie:** a debt, part-paid, in public, with a true answer declined. He owes her the truth about what he is and has not paid it. She owes him nothing and has said so and has been honest about what he cost her on Friday morning.
- **Elias / Doreen Sarn:** the only relationship in the volume in which somebody has told him something he did not know, and the only one in which he gave something away that was not a document.
- **Elias / Anja:** authorisation without information, and now four conditions in a card the size of a small envelope, and a telephone call to Northglass that he will never know the content of.
- **Dael / Anja:** eleven years and four lines about a shutter, and a docket neither of them can sign.
- **Mara / Anja:** a clause in the margin of a standing instruction, initialled and dated, written on a Sunday by a person who is not an officer, and neither of them has mentioned it and both of them will.

# Character State — after Batch 0003 (Chapters 21–30)

Read against `state/continuity.md`, which is the authority. Ages are canon. States as of the close of Chapter 30 / Saturday 7 November 2024, 19:00.

## Elias Venn, 32

**Where he is now.** Suspended from night duty since 07:00 on Sunday 11 October, in the building on a 07:00–15:00 and 18:00–21:00 rhythm. Permitted: ground floor, service lane, loading bay and loading-bay office, security room, front desk. Barred: Hall of Former Faiths, Gallery of Unnamed Hands, conservation floor, Quiet Wing. **He has been inside both barred galleries twice since 26 October and both times by signing a visitors' book as a member of the public, and the second time it was a public procedure with a statutory footing, and a Registrar noticed and said nothing in front of the room and everything in a corridor afterwards.** He stands in the corridor outside the Hall most days. He is a witness-guide in his own staff post for the first time in his working life, on a Saturday, in front of fifty-one people, and it was the object of an Annexe E stay.

**What he has, as of 7 November.** A pocket notebook with ninety-one lines in it. A police custody record with a crime number. **A cautioned statement in a police file in Low Ward, of which paragraph six is the word *reading*.** A witness list of ten groups in a plastic wallet, **with two names struck off in his own hand without a reason.** A memorandum on Museum notepaper with a movement number on it. A claim file that other people now hold copies of. A tape he cannot alter one figure of. **A form in a safe in Irving Quay that he is not allowed to write a word of and has never signed.** And the number — **ninety-four — which he said out loud, once, in a room, on a tape, on Monday 26 October at 20:22, with ninety-two and two, and has never said it since.**

**Skill state. Stage 1 — Echo, and now with three reads and one uncontrolled event he did not cause.** He asked a lamp who carried it out of the last building it was in and got a kitchen. He asked a bell whose hands and whether the sheet made them do it or they made it do the sheet, and got a pay office and a third person he cannot name. He read a sealed box with no question and got ninety seconds of a date in a hard hand and a tray marked *second post, three days*. **He asked one question of a bell in front of fifty-one people and the object answered a man's voice and went out of him into a child.** He cannot ask a room. He cannot stop a reach. He has no procedure for a child.

**The flaw, on the page, and it is changing shape.** For a month it was *the only place a fact lives*. Chapter 23: he strikes two names off a witness list in his own hand without a reason, and one of them is the only person in the city who has said a thing to him he did not already know. Chapter 25: he says a number in a room. Chapter 29: he gives the two flags in a municipal library, one of which he only half believes. Chapter 30: **he does not demand a count from a woman who has refused one, and the thing he has learned is that this is the first of his five refusals to ask that has cost him nothing, and that the pattern is the coat pocket, and that Doreen Sarn told him about it in October and he has taken a month to be able to use her own sentence.** The new flaw on the page is: *he has learned to be the man in the room instead of the man with the fact, and the man in the room is a better man and is not safer, and on Saturday morning he had fifty-one people in a public gallery who had all heard the same sentence and were all now in a document he had made.*

**The five times he did not ask**, recorded because they are now a pattern and Doreen Sarn would call it a method: the field officer's question about the two (Ch 25); Hattie, about the two people in her room (Ch 25); Dael, about the figure (Ch 27); Mara, about the form (Ch 29); Hattie, about a count (Ch 30). **Four of the five cost him something. The fifth cost nothing and he noticed, and noticing is the change.**

**Costs unpaid and still owed.** The Chapter 10 lie to Mara's face, unrepaired and not to be repaired by an apology. The Chapter 16 street. **Four seconds of a name.** A police record with the word *reading* in it. Two names on a list he struck off and did not tell anybody about. A bell under a stay he caused by asking a question in a corridor on 19 October. And the thing nobody has said to him yet: **that the stay's dispute route is his own form, and that a request he lodged to get an inspection is now the mechanism that keeps a bell unhandleable in a public room for an unknown period.**

**What he is frightened of, specifically.** Not the object and not the number. **That he has been the mechanism twice** — once for the stay and once, if it turns out, for the disposal — and that in about a year a person he has never met will read a form with his initials on it and decide that the man who asked for the inspection caused the thing the inspection found.

## Mara Iles, 28

**What she has done in this batch, in order, and none of it was a favour.** She built and posted the occupation form and **will not let him write a word of it and did not sign it either and said why**: *the form says may be left blank and it is not in the form to protect him, it is in the form to protect me from me.* She took it to a firm of solicitors in Irving Quay because a director's file is a bin and a firm's document schedule is not. She re-certified the Hall in one sentence twice and said out loud that a certificate is a statement that nobody has damaged anything since she last looked. She signed a five-clause protective-movement instruction **and wrote the risk on it herself**: *in the Registry's judgement it may be a removal from public view, and if it is, then this Museum has done it, and I am signing it anyway and I would like it recorded that I signed it.* **She built the Manifest Transfer Schedule out of the 1974 carbon instead of the box's label, and told him on the Tuesday that it was not an act of courage, it was administration.** She told the Director about the tour on a Sunday in ninety seconds in a corridor. She sent eleven letters in her own person and not on the Director's letterhead, and wrote down, in writing, **that the Museum would not telephone five households to ask whether a woman of eighty-three was alive.** She read out at a public meeting that she was not there for the Director and would not give an opinion. She said *yes, somebody did, about a Tuesday morning*, to a child of about nine, in a public gallery, and did not know what it would do and did it anyway.

**Her two refusals, both new and both on the page.** She refuses to let the box be opened in a room with a camera in it by anybody, including the woman it belongs to, and she says the reason: *the paper is not the point, the paper is the excuse.* And she refuses to be his witness on 26 October, in front of eleven people and a field officer, in advance.

**What is now in her file.** A schedule of contents that names a gate book. A printed payroll-bureau reply that pays off clause seven. A visitors' book with a suspended night guard's initials in it against his own request. A consent statement she did not write. A note dated 27 October recording **the day he did not ask her about the form**, which she keeps because in four years it will be the only evidence that he kept to it.

**What she still has not done.** She has still not had the reading. He has still not asked her for it. **And she has still not been told about the second envelope, and clause six is still live, and the four photographs of the erased card are still on the fourth floor of a building he cannot enter.**

## Ivo Pell, 25

**What he has done in this batch, and it is the batch's institutional spine.** He put up a notice board, a handrail, and a **rule about assistance at evacuation that shut the Hall of Former Faiths to every school group in the city on a Wednesday afternoon** and never told anybody it would. He read two safety files he had no business reading, put nine ticks in a column, and **refused to take the list down on his own** because a man of forty-four would work out in nine seconds that the entry which breaks it hardest is a suspended night guard's fixed position. He made a cover out of a pallet collar and a fire blanket. He wrote down the line for the case where nobody can say the word and said, in advance, *I will say it, and I am not going to be all right about saying it.* He said the word in a corridor at 01:01 on 25 October and it was **hold**.

**His exposure has become real.** He handed four of the 246 exposures from his March condition survey of the Gallery's flat files to the police, **in his own working capacity, unasked, and asked for it to be written down that way** — and the distinction he is relying on, that a scheduled Museum survey disclosed is not a member of staff photographing collections material, has never been tested, and Standing Instruction 14 clause five is a disciplinary matter, and **Anja Ward has to decide and has not.** He is on the fault report of 10 October. He has a sister with a chest. He has a Saturday systems day and a Tuesday that the rule shut.

**What he is owed.** On the Tuesday afternoon in an empty gallery, Elias Venn said *the woman in this room* and Ivo said *you have had four minutes with a bell and you have not had four minutes with her, and that is the actual bug in it, and it is not in the object.* **He has not been asked to do anything about it and he is going to be.**

## Dael Ordway, 44

**What he has done.** He came on at 17:10 on 21 October and found that his one card on the table had been turned over by a man of twenty-five with a piece of plywood, and said *I have been in this building eleven years and I have never had a card turned over that fast.* He found out on 28 October that the clause he wrote himself in October would lapse on the twenty-ninth, and told Nunn about it before he was told by anybody, and said *I have spent eleven years finding out what I would do on a Tuesday afternoon.* He was a witness for nine minutes in Cattlemarket and **did not mention Elias Venn's name once**, because the moment he does, Elias is a witness in a claim he is not a party to for the rest of his life. He took a private word from a solicitor in a corridor at twenty to four and came out with **a quantity of money that was not an offer**, and told Elias he would say he did not remember it, and that it would not be a lie and they would both know it was not a lie, and that he was not going to be a man who has been told things by the man in the middle of it. **He has not told Mara Iles that Elias was refused evidence, and Mara will find out on Friday, and he has warned Elias about it, and it cost him more to do it than it cost Elias to hear it.**

**His liability position is now documented in a transcript in Northglass and he cannot un-sign it.** He is not an ally and is not going to become one. He thinks the field is fair.

## Anja Ward, 53

**What she has lost, and how.** She lost the Hall on 21 October, by not being in a corridor, to a rule a systems technician put up with four lines and a plywood board. She was told about the tour on Sunday 27 October afternoon in ninety seconds, in a corridor, by a registrar who is not an officer, and then four seconds late in her own doorway by a suspended night guard who worked out that he had to tell her himself, and she made him understand the difference. She lost the object on 7 November, at 13:10, to a form she did not write, read into the record by a statutory officer with a duty, and **she did not sign it, did not argue with it, does not know how to stop it, and is not wrong**, and she said so in a corridor in three sentences, all of them about procedure, and said *I do not know how to stop it. That is not a thing I say often.*

**What she has still not done.** She has to decide whether Ivo's March condition survey, disclosed to the police, is a disciplinary matter under clause five. She has not told the Board about clause six. She has not set the fourth Monday. And she wrote four of the conditions on 15 October and one of them has taken a bell out of her gallery for an unknown period, and she wrote it, and nobody is going to argue that with her.

## Doreen Sarn, 78

**What she has done in this batch.** She came to a loading bay on the Thursday afternoon because Elias gave her a reason that was not *I want to show you a letter* — he had a document from a chief clerk in a payroll bureau that appeared to answer a question she has carried since 1974. She read it four times and then said, with a finger on the paragraph: **on the basis of a record of that kind I will not treat a sealed item as destroyed.** She said the phrase *somebody opens the box* in a loading bay at 15:41 on 29 October, at the same second as the man opposite her. She refused to be added to a witness list in Cattlemarket on the twenty-eighth and asked to be a member of the public instead: *the second I stand up in that room I become a piece of paper, and I've been that, and it cost me the rest of my life.* She said that if the envelope is opened without her in it, then the first account of what is in it will be somebody else's.

**Her three refusals stand and none is reversed.** She will not surrender the cash book. She will not read it to a lawyer, a trustee, a Board or a museum. She will not sign anything in an office. **And now a fourth: she will not be a witness.**

**What is now against her.** A registered letter from the Museum, signed for by a niece who is post, **face down on a sideboard since Wednesday 29 October.** A box in bay 9 with her envelope in it. A date in her own hand on the corner of it, in an echo, that says she was right to want it dated. **And a form with a blank in it, in a safe in Irving Quay, that somebody has built for a night cleaner and not for her.** **On Friday 27 November, the day after the hearing, the envelope opens in her front room at four o'clock in the afternoon with a fire on, and she is in the room, and no Museum employee is, and she has not been told that either.**

## Hattie Bramm, 54

**Her three refusals, all standing, none reversed.** She refused a microphone on 7 November and did not refuse the room, and gave the reason in front of eleven adults and a filmmaker: *if I stand up there with a microphone then this morning is a thing that happened; if I sit here with a laptop then this morning is a Friday.* She refused, on 26 October, in front of two recorders, to do a sum — *a number that can be checked is a number that can be stopped* — and **no total was stated in that room by anybody.** She refused, on 29 October, to be the speaker, and to give the hundred and forty names, and said why in a document: *a list of a hundred and forty names is the only thing in this business that can't be a receipt and can't be an exhibit and can't be a schedule of contents, and the second somebody has it in a file it stops being a list of people and starts being evidence.*

**The thing she did that nobody else in the volume could do.** She came to a clearing site for the ninth time in thirty-one years and was right about four inches of water in front of twenty men and a boy of fourteen, and nobody thanked her and she asked not to be thanked: *if the sum total of what I am is a woman who was right about a pallet, then I have spent thirty-one years being the gate clerk at a gate that did not exist.* And she made a foreman read a bay number out loud twice so that a man with a clipboard wrote it down and she could date it.

**The hardest thing she did, and it was deliberate.** **The stop word is *next* and it is hers and only hers**, and on 7 November she was the only person in a public gallery who could have stopped an object reaching a nine-year-old boy, and **she did not say it, and could not say it, because saying it stops the count**, and a man of sixty-one in overalls had said *that's the shop* twice and a row of children were adding numbers to a gate on a wet morning in 1974, and she would not end it. **She typed the run and typed the gaps in the gaps and cried and worked and would not stop.** She has not yet been asked four minutes of anything. **She is owed a conversation and it has not happened and the reader knows it and he has not had it.**

**What she is owed and has not got.** A conversation. A say in whether the hundred and forty names ever come out of the drawer. **And a letter addressed to the man at number 14 that she took in on Friday morning and carried in her coat all day and did not open.**

## Perry Loach, 61 (prose: *a night cleaner in his early sixties*)

**Where he is in the batch, and it is the whole of the batch.** He was on the floor of a loading bay at 00:50 on 25 October with eleven people in a room and was in it because **nobody had told him the room had anything in it** — no clause in the standing instruction, the protocol, the form or the docket says the room is empty, and a man on a Saturday shift with a mop is in every room on the floor. He was on the floor of the Hall of Former Faiths on 7 November and was not on the rota and **came because he asked Mara Iles on a telephone whether Saturdays were still on.** He was a full hour into the sequence before he spoke, and then he said **"that's the shop"** and said it again, because a thing said once in a room of fifty-one people is a private incident and a thing said twice is a record: the fitters' shop, eleven men on the roll on that Friday morning, the foreman wanting to know if they were all in because they were not all in, and the last time that bell ever made a noise. He put the mop down against a wall with both hands, the way you put a thing down when you have had it for eleven years and you do not want to drop it.

**He has still not been asked one question by anybody about what happened to him**, and he has now, on the record, in a public gallery, in front of press, put a number into a tape. **He wants it asked in front of the people that was in it. He does not want anybody to ask him if he has remembered it right, and he said it once and would not say it twice.** He is the reason the public room was survivable and the reason it was necessary and he does not know either of those things. **The numeral sixty-one does not appear in Chapter 30 and does not appear in the same paragraph as his name anywhere in Batch 0003.**

## Aurelia Roth, 34 — Registry assessor

Named the object **Annexe D** on 19 October. Held the **return inspection on 26 October** with a member of the public present, read the six-word reason off his own form out loud and said in terms that it was the only request in six years that had been both complete and true when it was written and that it was going to be a problem in about six weeks. **She was right and she has been proved right**, and she said the thing about the Registry's material-change procedure that a disturbance report names the requester as the reporting party, and Ottilie Grange said the same thing four days later about the same clause, and **two competent statutory officers with no interest in this case have independently arrived at the volume's argument and neither of them has ever seen a piece of its evidence.**

## Ottilie Grange — Registry assessor

The assessor of 12 October who photographed the hand-cut plinth card with a scale rule and described the erasure as **partial** and stopped there, because partial was what the thing was. She read **Annexe E** into the record on 7 November at 13:10, under procedure 11, on a disturbance report, in about four minutes, and she did not argue with anybody, and she told them it was correct, and she told them the dispute route is the requester, and she told them in terms that this is not an accident of drafting. **She is not a friend of anybody in this building and she has been right about everything and it has cost a bell its hands.**

## Wynne Loach, 34 — night bus conductor, Perry's daughter

Introduced 23 October by a form and 26 October in person. Lodged **QA/CR/1420** at the front desk on **23 October** with the six-word reason **does anything here reach a person**, walked into a restricted gallery on a public procedure with a member of the public present, and asked the only direct question anybody has asked Elias Venn in a month: **can you ask it, is there a thing there, is that what you do.** He answered yes, and said it is not a demon and not a saint and does not seek worship, and that its demonstrated function is a count spoken aloud where the counted can hear it, and that he has been wrong about it four times in a month. **A field officer wrote it on a form.** She is the reason the object became a *disturbance* in a document, because two requesters is a pattern and one is an inspection. She has not been asked anything since and she has not gone away.

## Vera Pilling, 81 — of 6 Sillick Lane

Was on the looms, eleven years, and came on her own on a bus and stood up at the side of a table at half past eleven on Friday 30 October without being asked, and said four things in ninety seconds, of which the second is the chapter: *there are two hundred and forty-odd households got a letter off that woman in the last month, and in thirty-one years I never had one, and I want it on the record, from a woman of eighty-one, that that's what this has been for me. Not the bell. Not the museum. The letter.* **She is the reason there is a speaker**, because Hattie Bramm refused to be one on the grounds that a first voice in the room has to be a person who stood on that gate and can say *we*. She is one of six of eleven. She is not on Hattie's six and has not been traced.

## Arthur Neve, 78 — Ravenscourt and Hale, 6 Northgate Steps

The estate's agent of 30 October 1974, with a chest since a pleurisy in 1969, a frame, a plastic carrier bag and a bad coat. Came to a hearing in Cattlemarket at half past four on 28 October because he saw the weighbridge photograph in the *Chronicle* on Sunday. **He is the only witness anybody has ever found who was there.** He carries the buyer's carbon of the same eleven items, in a different hand, in a different order, with a line at the foot reading *received, all, subject to weight*, and **item 11: *clock, works office, stopped 05:54*** — the number in a reading, written by a hand that did not know it was writing evidence. He will produce it on **26 November** and not before, and his reason is the volume's reason: a man of seventy-eight with a carbon and a bad chest is a witness, and a witness is a document. He has told Elias Venn that he has been the agent, emptied the site, took no shilling, and thought about a lamp more than he has thought about most things.

## H. Lound — Chief Clerk, Remittances, Municipal Payroll Bureau

Wrote **MPB/R/8841** in about forty seconds on a Thursday morning because a registrar in a museum asked a question that was not about a man named Case, and said the sentence the whole of this business is built on: **a bureau's remittance record for a person is a record of the person and of money paid and is not a record of the employer.** He has never heard of the Morrowgate Museum of Quiet Saints, he has no interest in it, and he has made a rule that a payroll clerk can put in front of a tribunal and win on. He is furniture and he is the most useful piece of furniture in the volume.

## The twenty-two hourlies, and the fourteen-year-old

Twenty men on the clearing site on 22 October, hourly, agency, in new boiler suits, with a clipboard, who **did not open anything** because nothing in it could be opened and because a man who opens a thing he has been asked to move is a man who has opened a thing. Two in the cab. One foreman, Upton. One **fourteen-year-old on a week's work experience from a school in Northgate**, in a designated exclusion zone for five hours, who was moved sixteen inches up a ramp on the strength of a telephone call from a call box and who then backed four feet to clear a hook and put his wrist on a sawn end of timber and got a **fracture of the radial styloid** and will be asked about it by every employer in this city for thirty years, and whose grandfather was a fitter at the Sillick, and who asked for that site by name in September, and who was back on it on the Thursday because there is a college tutor with a form that says the first aider is a named person on the site induction record, and she was not near him because the ambulance came in nine minutes and the fitter got there first. **Nobody is to blame. That is the sentence.**

## The minor working people, added and extended in Batch 0003

**Fenn** · **Priest** · **Spence** (metals) · **Nunn** (transfer clerk; *Christ, that's brilliant*, and *I never opened it, and it was never in my gift*) · **Petrie** (Nunn's assistant) · **Wilkes** (conservation officer, eleven years, who strikes *unpitched at rest* out of every form in the building and says the word was in the template before the objects were) · **Sergeant Ashby** and a PC, Low Ward · **VPC Ainsley**, duty solicitor · **Cheryl Vosper** (proved right) · **Redgate and Kell** (four questions) · **Casey**, of the Hill office, with a folder and a lanyard and the specific disappointment of a person who has arranged a thing and is not going to be in the picture · **Field Officer Marsh** and her assistant · **Upton** · **Halloway Crane Hire** (a stencilled number on a crane cab) · **A hall porter named on a docket and in a fire register** · **MALLOY, J. — Grade 2 — 2,041 hrs — NIL** (furniture, and the only figure a reading gave in Chapter 25, and a man's own hours).

## Relationships, end of Batch 0003

- **Elias / Mara:** professional respect hardened into a partnership of instruments. **He told the Director about the tour himself because she told him, in a corridor, that he would never be trusted with an unshared fact again.** He still has not asked her for the reading. She has still not been told about the second envelope. There is no more private channel and it is the arrangement and it is the relationship and it is not going to be sentimental.
- **Elias / Ivo:** working friendship that has now been *he gave me the systems note and you gave me the police* and *you have had four minutes with a bell and not four minutes with her.* He still does not ask Ivo for anything. Ivo still does not wait to be asked. **The two of them have never once been in a room with each other in a crisis and this batch has given them three.**
- **Elias / Dael:** deferred-to, managed, and then **mutually declined.** Dael will not say what a solicitor said to him in a corridor and Elias will not ask, and both of them know, and both of them know that the other knows, and neither of them has said so. **It is not an alliance and it is not going to become one and it is the most functional relationship in the building.**
- **Elias / Hattie:** a debt that has been paid three times and is not paid. He stood in a public gallery and said a word in a room — *the woman in this room* — that has put her in a tape without her name in it, and has not yet asked her permission for any of it, and knows the number of minutes he has to do it in.
- **Elias / Doreen Sarn:** the only relationship in the volume in which somebody has told him something he did not know, and the only one in which he has said a sentence out loud at the same second as she did, and it was *somebody opens the box.* **He will not be in the room when it happens and that was his own line and it cost him the only thing he has ever wanted to do.**
- **Elias / Anja:** authorisation without information for a month, and then on 27 October a man on a restriction telling a director something she already knew, four seconds late, on purpose, and being told that the difference mattered. **And on 7 November a director losing to a form and saying so.**
- **Mara / Anja:** a clause in the margin, and now a Sunday in a corridor in ninety seconds, and neither of them has mentioned it and both of them will.
- **Mara / Doreen Sarn:** eleven days and one meeting, and they agree about the object and about the room and about the camera, and neither of them likes it.
- **Hattie / Doreen Sarn:** one phone call, and a document schedule at Irving Quay, and neither has met the other and both know the other's name.
