# Character State — after Batch 0004 (Chapters 31–40)

Read against `state/continuity.md`, which is the authority. Ages are canon. States as of the close of Chapter 40 / Thursday 19 November 2024, 23:00.

## Elias Venn, 32

**Where he is now.** Suspended from night duty since 07:00 on Sunday 11 October 2024, on a 07:00–15:00 and 18:00–21:00 rhythm that is a rhythm and not a job, and which he works past on the evenings because the letters take until twenty to eleven. Permitted: ground floor, service lane, loading bay, loading-bay office, security room, front counter. Barred: Hall of Former Faiths, Gallery of Unnamed Hands, conservation floor, Quiet Wing. **In this batch he entered the Hall of Former Faiths once, as a member of the public, ref QA/CR/1187, signed in at 10:26 on 19 November, and a Gallery twice — once through a doorway and once because a registrar opened a drawer in it with a key in front of nine people — and the security room once, where the only restricted file in the building he may legally open is, and he opened the attachment envelope in it.**

**What he has, as of 19 November.** A pocket notebook, **a hundred and fifteen pages, about a hundred and ten written**, and **an inventory of it, written on the back of a bay-check sheet on Monday the sixteenth, with seven items, of which item 7 is *the notebook itself, nobody in this building has read it, Mara Iles has asked for it and I have not said no, and that is worse*.** A police custody record and a cautioned statement whose paragraph six is the word *reading*. A witness list with two names struck off in his own hand without a reason. A form in a safe in Irving Quay he has never signed. **A registered letter from the Museum, signed for by a niece who is post, in a coat pocket, unopened, that he has not read and has not asked to read.** **A form with a box on it for a period, twenty-eight days from the date of determination, which expires on 17 December 2024, which he has told nobody he has, and which Aurelia Roth wrote the date of on the back of her own copy in pencil and refused to send him.** And a work authorisation with T. VENN on it, which is in a red lever-arch file on a shelf in the security room where he put it back himself, and the shelf's own sheet says 16:52 and does not say what he took out.

**Skill state. Stage 1 — Echo, unchanged, and unexercised for ten days.** **There is no reading in this batch. There is no reach, no uncontrolled event of his own, and no object asked anything.** What changed is not the ability. It is that he now knows, on the page, in his own words, that the ability is not a gift: *it is a room he was in, in the last week of October 2006, at fourteen, and something in it fused to him.* **He has been the fourteen-year-old in this volume since 10 October and has not once known it, and the document in the security room did not tell him anything about the ability and told him everything about himself.**

**The flaw, on the page, and it is changing shape again.** For a month it was *the only place a fact lives*. In Batch 0003 it became *he has learned to be the man in the room instead of the man with the fact*. **In Batch 0004 it is: he has now told a stranger in a room almost everything and has still not handed a woman in his own building a hundred and fifteen pages, and the reason is a promise he made to himself in a laundrette, and his mother has told him that the reason and the promise are the same thing with a different word on it.** And underneath that, new: **he concealed a fact for four days to protect the building, for the first time, and it worked, and he did not tell a single person.**

**The seven times he did not ask**, and the sixth and the seventh are this batch's. The field officer, for a name for the two. Hattie, about the two people in her room. Dael, about the figure. Mara, about the form. Hattie, for a count. **Doreen Sarn, for the address, until Thursday 12 November, five weeks and one day after the sixth of October.** **Hattie Bramm, again, after she had handed the only sentence the case has to somebody else.** **Six cost him something. The seventh cost nothing and he noticed, and noticing is now the pattern, and the pattern is the coat pocket.**

**Costs unpaid and still owed.** The Chapter 10 lie to Mara's face, unrepaired and not to be repaired by an apology. The Chapter 16 street. Four seconds of a name. A police record with the word *reading* in it. Two names on a list he struck off. **A stay on a bell he caused by asking a question at a front desk in October, and now a public register entry he caused the same way, and now a twenty-eight-day period that expires on 17 December and that he has told nobody about.** And the false *no* in a solicitor's office in Irving Quay.

**What he is frightened of, specifically.** Not the object and not the number. **That he has now been the mechanism three times, and that the third one is a date with his own form number on it, and that in about a year a person he has never met will read a form with his initials on it and decide that the man who asked for the inspection caused the thing the inspection found, and will be right.**

## Mara Iles, 28

**What she has done in this batch.** She took the seller's carbon out of drawer 6B herself at 08:20 on Saturday 14 November with a key, in a public gallery, with nine people in it, put a slip in with it, carried it to Irving Quay in a grey archival wallet, and **put the slip and the sheet back into drawer 6B together at about four o'clock that afternoon before she left the building — which is the only place the Museum's copy of that carbon exists and where Hattie Bramm read it on the Sunday.** *(An earlier version of this line said only "and put it back before she left", against a Chapter 35 that never said she did; Chapter 35 now says it in one sentence and this line and the prose agree.)* She sat in a room in Irving Quay with a member of the public and a witness of seventy-eight and **read a document out loud, twice, so that two fifty-year-old carbons could be compared, and then she had to listen to him say no to a question that was asked in the ordinary way.** She typed *take no instructions* into a Museum schedule at half past nine on Saturday night and it took an accession number and it has a location. **She finally told the Director about the second envelope**, in a doorway, in about ninety seconds, on a printed form with a print run of four hundred, and **she found out about it on Friday 14 November at about half past four in a doorway from Elias Venn and did not ask him for it in the doorway and did not ask him for the reading and has still not asked him for the reading.** She refused to help him frame the question on the 17 December form because *the moment I help you with it, it is the Registry that asked whether an object reaches a person, and it never will be.* She did not go into the Board minute's room. She read the name out because she wrote it and said so in the minute and said in a corridor that she would do it again on a Saturday in the conservation office, on her own, in a hurry, with nobody asking her to.

**Her two refusals of Batch 0003 stand and are not restaged**: she will not be his witness, and she will not open the box in a room with a camera in it. **In this batch she has added a third position, which is that she will not take a number out of a notebook even if it is given to her, because the number came from an object she cannot explain and a file she cannot open.** And a fourth, softer and harder: she has not asked for the notebook and has not stopped asking.

**What is now in her file.** A schedule of contents that names a gate book. A certified comparison of two carbons, one of which is in a plastic wallet in Northglass. A typed four-clause line from a 1974 fee ledger with an accession number and a location. A Board minute that names a porter. A visitors' book with a suspended night guard's initials in it against his own request. A box of her own that says *because I was not asked*.

**What she has still not done.** She has still not had the reading. She has still not seen the notebook. **And she is now within nine days of being a woman who has to sit at a table at 18:00 on the twenty-sixth with a field officer and a £140 fee and a determination on a public register, and she has never been given one number that came out of an object.**

## Ivo Pell, 25

**What he has done in this batch, and it is the batch's institutional spine in a twenty-five-year-old's two hands.** He booked a Registry attendance for the Gallery's re-certification off the weekly systems bulletin, without being asked, as he did in October — **and this time nobody had to tell Elias afterwards, because Elias was in the building and the booking was on a screen at a public counter.** He changed the word *amended* to *relocated* in a systems index because a lawyer asking who moved a Schedule 12 object must not be answered with a suspended night guard's name. He told Jokes about a weighbridge poster with no caption on it and then explained that the joke was the only thing he had. **He got Hattie Bramm's four minutes, and he wrote them down on a systems sheet in ballpoint on his knee, and he gave the sheet to Elias Venn without reading him the sentence, and he told the Director he had done it in his own working capacity and had wanted it written down that way.** He took a decision in writing that gives him no disciplinary record and takes away his ability to be alone in a room with a camera, and he said in a corridor that the sensible thing is that the thing he did was right, and he has been sick about it since October, and now he has been given permission and **he does not want the permission**, and that is the sentence his sister says about her chest.

**His exposure is decided and it is worse than a clean bill and better than a hearing.** No disciplinary action, on the ground that a scheduled survey is the reason and not the photographer. **A condition from 1 December: countersignature at the point of making, every time.** He will not like it in three weeks when he has to find a supervisor at half past ten on a Monday. He asked the Director to be told that the thing he said through her door was him and not the wall.

**What he is still owed.** On the Tuesday afternoon in an empty gallery in October, Elias Venn said *the woman in this room* and Ivo said *you have had four minutes with a bell and you have not had four minutes with her, and that is the actual bug in it, and it is not in the object.* **In Chapter 36 he had the four minutes, on a Sunday, in a gallery, with a rule of his own making keeping him in the room, and he was not prepared for it, and he wrote it down afterwards, and he gave it away rather than keep it, and the man who should have had it was two floors down at a public counter.**

## Dael Ordway, 44

**What he has done in this batch.** He put item five on a Board sub-group paper on 9 October, with a minute note that the material is unavailable because a proposed disposition is before the standing instruction — **and the last of those dispositions expired at 17:00 on Wednesday 18 November while he was not in the room and the room did not know.** He said the thing about Elias Venn to **Nunn**, the transfer clerk, in a bay at about half past four on a Thursday, word for word, and Nunn carried it down two flights and gave it to Elias on the Sunday because Elias asked him once not to improve on anybody. He has not been on nights since 6 November and has not been the supervisor since the seventh. He has not mentioned it to Elias and will not, and Elias has not gone up to him and made a speech about it.

**His sentence, verbatim, and it is the truest thing anybody says about Elias in the batch:** *there is a man on a seven-to-three and a six-to-nine rhythm who is not on the payroll and not on the rota, and who has been inside more barred rooms in this building this month than any member of staff, and there are days when I have to write down whether he was in the building, and I have started writing it, and I have started writing it because a suspended man is not a thing I can put in a report, and I do not want to be the man who has a column with his name in it* — and then: *I am not saying it to be fair to you. I am saying it because I have got a liability position in a transcript in Northglass that I cannot carry by myself, and if the day comes that somebody wants to know how many times that door opened in November, the answer is going to be in a column, and I would rather it was in a column with my name in it than a column with nobody's.*

**He is not an ally and he is not going to become one and the third way they have of being with each other is mutual declined, and it is the most functional relationship in the building.** He was not in the room where the name was read out. He is on the paper that put it there.

## Anja Ward, 53

**What she has decided in this batch, and both are in writing.** **Clause five: no disciplinary action, on the ground that the survey was scheduled and indexed and would have been made anyway — and a condition from 1 December that destroys the only thing that made him safe, and she says out loud that she is aware of it and would like it recorded that she is aware of it.** She refused to back-date it to the fifteenth of October, which is the date she should have decided it, and said why: *in about four years somebody is going to read this file and work out that a director sat on it for a month, and they might as well work that out from me.*

**She read out her own card of 20 October, in a corridor, before she put it in a drawer** — a card that has been on her table for four weeks and that calls the man she is deciding the review of a procedure that has not got a name — and she put it in a drawer dated the seventeenth, not the twentieth, because the reservation has four more weeks of evidence under it. **She refused to sign the disclosure form on the spot and gave a date: Friday, or a written reason on Friday**, and her reason is that a Director who signs things on the spot is a Director who is managing the paperwork instead of running an institution, and she watched two of them do it and one of them is her and she did it for nine years and it took a bell.

**What she has set.** **The review is on Wednesday 25 November 2024 at 09:25, half an hour, minuted, a copy to him — and it is not a disciplinary hearing, not a capability, and not a hearing under the standing instruction, because there is no incident to refer and clause seven has nothing to bite on.** The noticeboard still says *fourth Monday* and she is not taking it down, because a document is corrected and a person is not. **And she has put it on the Wednesday, the day before six households are in a room, on purpose, because on Thursday the twenty-sixth she wants a file with something in it.**

**What she said that is the sentence the volume is built on, at the Board, in answer to *who is observing it?*** **Whoever is in the room. That is the mechanism. It has been the mechanism for two hundred years in this city and it is the reason the buildings are free, and it is not a metaphor — the people who come in on a wet Saturday and stand in front of a case and look at something for ninety seconds are doing it, and they do not know they are doing it, and if they stopped, it would stop.** And she would not let the word into a minute, because the word would be accurate and a minute is a public document.

**What she still has not done.** She has still not told the Board about clause six. **She has still not decided whether to appeal an Annexe E stay she lost to a form she did not write, and Aurelia Roth has said in a public gallery that she would want to know before the end of the day if anybody in the building was about to lodge one without telling her.** She has still not opened locker 81, she has still not opened the unlisted room, she has not been in the Gallery, and **she has now had a determination made about an object in her gallery that has put it on a public register.**

## Doreen Sarn, 78

**What she has done in this batch, and it is the batch's hinge.** She was asked, properly, for the address, five weeks and one day after the sixth of October, and she had been waiting for the question since the sixth and said so. **She said a street out loud: Quill & Frayle, Solicitors, 14 Lincoln's Lane, Irving Quay**, from a brass plate, and she remembered the firm and the street and not the number, and **the number is on the 1974 receipt in a drawer in Civic Hill and has been since the seventh of November 1974, and nobody in fifty years had ever asked who had written to that firm.** She explained why she wrote to them: a list off the mill's yearbook against the words *acting for the purchaser*, and *you don't write to a company, a company isn't a person, you can write to a man.* And she said the envelope contained one sheet, a copy of a pay sheet, copied out of the gate book and the weekly sheet and the wage book on a Thursday night in November 1974 at her own table, on a carbon from the office.

**She said the leaving card out loud, unprompted, so that nobody else could do it for him: MALLOY, J. — Grade 2 — 2,041 hrs — NIL.** *That is one man's own hours on one grade on his own card and NIL is what he was handed in a brown envelope and that is what a leaving card is. It is not a wage. It is not a headcount. It is not a list. If you take those three words and put them next to anything else in this business you will have made a man who worked thirty-one years into a number in a box, and he is seventy-one or he is dead and either way he is not yours to do that to.*

**She turned the coat pocket on him, because he quoted it back at her and she would not let him have it as a thing she said and he used.** *A book with one reader is a book that can lose itself, because a book with one reader is not a record, it is a habit, and the moment somebody has to prove what was in it, the habit's not evidence, it is an opinion.* And then she pointed at her own cash book: a list of one household, on a shelf, in a room, that she has told nobody in nine years.

**She was told the date.** Friday the twenty-seventh of November, four o'clock, her own front room, a gas fire on, two conciliation officers, a supervisor and a camera, no Museum employee and no Elias Venn. **She said *that's the first thing you've said in two minutes that I believe, and I'll tell you why — everybody who's ever come in here about that envelope has wanted to be in the room.*** And she pushed the registered letter across the table and told him to take it away and not tell her what it says, because if he tells her then she has known and her three weeks is a different three weeks. She will open it on Friday night after the box has been open.

**Her four refusals all stand and none has moved.** The cash book. Reading it to a lawyer, a trustee, a Board or a museum. Signing anything in an office. Being a witness. **And she has now added the fifth by refusing, on Saturday afternoon, to let a four-clause line from a 1974 fee ledger be written down in her own hand: Elias types it, Mara files it, and the index will say *a Museum record* and nobody will ever know where it came from.**

## Hattie Bramm, 54

**She has had her four minutes, and she did not have them from the man with the notebook.** On Sunday 15 November, at about half past one, she came up to the Gallery of Unnamed Hands on her own because it was a Sunday and the Friday queue was the first since the seventh, and the only member of staff on that floor was Ivo Pell, and Ivo's own rule — *a person on their own is a party* — meant he could not leave. She read the 1974 receipt standing up at plinth 9, a document from that mill she had never seen in nine years, and at about 14:00 she said the sentence the whole of this business is going to need: **the hundred and forty are everybody she can put to a street; the ones she cannot get are the people who never had a letter; there is no list of those and there never will be, because you cannot take a list of a thing nobody kept.** And then: **if anybody stands her up on the twenty-sixth she will say that, and then it will be in a file instead of a gallery, and that is the reason she has not said it for nine years.**

**And she asked whether the Museum is writing to the hundred and forty, and he did not know, and she said *no, they won't*, in a voice that was not disappointed.**

**She has still not refused anything in this batch and she has not had to.** Her three refusals stand: the microphone, the sum, the speaker, the hundred and forty. **And she has now said, in a room, the one thing she has been refusing to say for nine years, to a systems technician, on a Sunday, for four minutes, and she is not on any list, and Elias Venn is two floors down at a counter and does not know it happened until ten past four, and it is the seventh time he has chosen not to ask and the first time the choice had a shape: he wanted the sentence, and she had given it away, and if he had asked her for it on Monday it would have been his.**

## Aurelia Roth, 34, and Ottilie Grange, Registry assessors

**Both were right in Chapter 40 and neither has seen a piece of the case's evidence and neither asked to.** Roth led the material-change inspection, found no material change in the condition or the classification, and then **refused the plain reading of Annexe E**, which is the batch's institutional centre: *if a stay lifted on a determination of no material change, then every stayed object in this city would lift the first time an inspector looked at it and found it stable. I am not going to improve that clause. The Registry does not write the schedule and every one of the four hundred copies of it has a 4(d) on it that somebody put there in about 1840 because they needed somewhere to put the true things that would not fit in the other boxes.* Grange put the disturbance in 4(d) because it is true and 4(c) cannot hold it. **The determination is a null and the stay stands and the register is public from 20 November.**

**Roth separated the appeal route from the material-change route in front of the room, unasked, because Anja Ward said in Chapter 30 that a no-finding at the end of eleven weeks would lift the stay, and it would not, because that is a different instrument.** And she said she would want to know before the end of the day if anybody in the building was about to lodge an appeal without telling her.

**And she gave Elias Venn the only dated instrument left in the business, in a public gallery, out loud, in front of six people, and refused to help him write the question, and wrote the date on the back of her own copy in pencil and would not send it, and said that if he does not use it she will regard that as the correct outcome.** She also said the sentence that undoes him: *you are the reason a determination about that object is going to be on a public register in this city in the morning, and I am sorry about that, and I do not think anybody is going to thank either of us.* And that in about three days a great many people are going to tell him that using his question was the mistake, and that both holding it and using it were correct.

## Arthur Neve, 78, of Ravenscourt and Hale, 6 Northgate Steps

**He came on Saturday 14 November at five past ten with a frame and a plastic carrier bag, having said no twice, and he was asked a third time and he came because the van had gone and the letter to a solicitor's office had already been written and typed.** He had been up since half past six. He brought the buyer's carbon and **kept it**: his hand stayed on the corner of it for two hours, he read his own side aloud where he could, and it went back into the plastic wallet into the carrier bag onto his knees and left with him up six steps to a bus, **because a doctor in 1971 told him not to sit in a small warm car with a man who is going to talk.** **It is his document until 26 November 2024 and on 26 November it is the conciliation service's document, in a room, with a field officer in it, and he said that out loud, in a solicitor's office, before anybody started.**

**What he gave the case is the sentence the whole of Movement 4 turns on, and he gave it to a man who had just tried to give him a number instead: *a carbon is a document and a document is a thing you can put in front of a person and say is this yours, yes or no. And what you've just described is not a document. What you've described is being in a room with a thing that talks.*** He said he would rather have come because of the carbon, and that a document is a thing you can put in front of a person, and that the plastic wallet is no use at all to a man of his age with a bad chest when it is raining.

## Emyr Rhys, 58, and Beverley Marsden, 62 — Quill & Frayle, 14 Lincoln's Lane

**Emyr Rhys, thirty-one years at the firm, the person who deals with what comes in.** He is correct and he is decent and being right is what costs a wage sheet its life. He will not hand over the clearance schedule, he will not explain why twice, and the reason is that it is a client's document and the partner who could give it has said no twice for a reason that is a reason. **He offered, unasked, the thing he could give: the firm's own destruction schedule, which is a public document in the sense that it exists and anybody can ask to see it.** And when a transcript clerk proposed a cross-reference, he did not treat it as a hole in a schedule — **he treated it as an ordinary cross-reference on a transmittal, of which about two hundred are signed every year and nobody ever fills the box in, and he filled it in, in ink, and it names D. SARN as the addressee and alive and it will be there in a hundred years.**

**Beverley Marsden, twenty-six years, a reading frame and a printed pro forma.** She reads two fifty-year-old carbons out loud, twice, from one to eleven and from the other end, the same way both times. **She produced the best argument in the chapter out of a pay-office stamp in a tin with a date on the die.** She certified the comparison and wrote on it what she could not certify: *three differences in wording, and one figure. I do not certify that either document is correct. I certify that this is a true comparison of the two documents as they stand.* And she wrote *none produced* in a box on a pro forma and initialled it, on a false answer, and did not know.

## H. Lound — Chief Clerk, Remittances, Municipal Payroll Bureau

**Furniture, and the most useful piece of furniture in the volume, and he is about to be asked to do the same thing twice.** He has never heard of the building and has no interest in it. On 29 October he wrote MPB/R/8841 in about forty seconds and gave the volume its method in a sentence. **On the Board's resolution of 18 November he is going to be handed a form with two questions on it, one of which he can answer out of a remittance record and one of which he cannot answer out of anything at all, and he is going to have to answer the second one with nothing, and sign it, and the nothing goes in a Museum file with the Museum's request number on it.**

## The paper merchant, and the bonded store

**Miss Arkwright**, bonded goods clerk, about thirty, six years, competent, courteous and completely blameless, who read a sheet and weighed four lots and put a new strap on a book, and who said *I have been doing this for six years and I have had about four hundred containers through those bays and I have never once known what was in one*, and *you have to say that one to somebody, and everybody in that chain has about nine hundred loads in their head.* **Mowbray**, who has a board and a marker and reads a seal number back. **Renwick**, fifty, who signs about eleven thousand consignment notes a year, has never opened one, will not start, does not know and was never going to know and cannot be told anything now. **A man on a compound in the wind with a pen for a fence and a mobile number, who is not the company.** **A merchant's clerk at a yard weighbridge on an approach who says the load is up on last week because the paper was damp, and is right not to think twice about it.** **Nobody in that chain is a villain and the man with the marker at 08:26 wrote a number in a box and the box had nothing above it, and it is a bin behind a loading bay with a hand truck and a sheet of paper at a thousand times the scale, and the man with the hand truck is a professional.**

## The minor working people, added and extended in Batch 0004

**Miss Arkwright** · **Mowbray** · **Renwick** · **Emyr Rhys** · **Beverley Marsden** · **Mrs Angela Vessey**, 64, trustee since 2019, chair of the sub-group, who reads headings and who read the Schedule 12 heading out loud in a room and is not a villain · **R.M.J.**, three initials in a 2006 file, nobody knows whose · **the paper merchant's four men** · **the Board Secretary**, who types a minute in twenty minutes and brings it down herself and says *it's not urgent* · **Priest**, who takes messages at the front desk and puts them on a board, and who put Hattie Bramm's message about drawer 6B on it on a Sunday

## Relationships, end of Batch 0004

- **Elias / Mara:** an arrangement hardened into something neither of them will name. **He has now told a stranger in a room almost everything and has still not handed her a hundred and fifteen pages**, and she has found out about the second envelope in a doorway and has not asked him for anything, and she typed the fee ledger for him at half past nine on a Saturday night and filed it with an accession number, and she refused to help him write the question on the form that expires on 17 December. **There is no more private channel and the only private channel left is the notebook, and both of them know that, and neither of them has said it.**
- **Elias / Ivo:** **the four minutes are paid and paid to the wrong man, and Ivo knows it, and Elias knows it, and neither of them says it.** Ivo changed a systems-index line and booked a Registry slot and got a disciplinary decision with letters on it and gave Hattie Bramm's sentence to a counter instead of keeping it. **The two of them have now been in a room together four times in five weeks and none of them was a crisis.**
- **Elias / Dael:** **mutual declined, third time, and it is the most functional relationship in the building.** Dael said the true thing about him to a transfer clerk and Nunn gave it to him, and Dael has not mentioned it and will not, and Elias has not gone up to him and made a speech about it, and Nunn has told Elias that if he makes a speech he will have wasted the only good thing anybody has done for him this month.
- **Elias / Hattie:** **a debt paid seven times and not paid.** He has her sentence, from a systems technician, on a systems sheet, in his coat pocket, and he has not asked her for it, and she is coming on the twenty-sixth and will be a member of the public and not a witness, and he knows that if anybody stands her up she will say the thing about the people who never had a letter and it will be in a file instead of a gallery.
- **Elias / Doreen Sarn:** **the only relationship in the volume in which somebody has told him something he did not know, twice, in five days.** He asked for the address and got a firm and a street. He gave her a date and she believed him because nobody had wanted to be in the room. She pushed a letter across a table and told him not to read it. **And she refused to let him write down four clauses in her own hand, which is the first time she has refused to be a record and it is the right refusal.**
- **Elias / Anja:** authorisation without information, then a decision with letters on it, then a card in a drawer read out in a corridor before it was filed. **And on 19 November he is in her gallery as a member of the public, in a coat, on the public side of a rope, asking one question, while the Director stands at the end of the row with a folder and loses a second time.**
- **Mara / Anja:** a clause in a margin, a Sunday in a corridor, a minute note under a Board paper, and now a review set for the Wednesday and a disclosure form that will be signed or refused on the Friday. **Neither of them has mentioned any of it and both of them will.**
- **Mara / Aurelia Roth:** a procedure that will not do what it was written to do, and a woman who is right about it twice, and neither of them is on the other's side and neither of them is the other one's enemy.

# Character State — after Batch 0005 (Chapters 41–50), the end of Volume 01

Read against `state/continuity.md`, which is the authority. Ages are canon. States as of the close of Chapter 50 / Sunday 13 December 2024, about 12:00.

## Elias Venn, 32

**Where he is now.** **No post and no access.** His appointment as night officer was **terminated with effect from Tuesday 8 December 2024** under clause 11 of the Museum's Constitution, **no fault being found and no right of appeal being conferred**, and he has been **barred from the building as from 1 December as a security matter**, save attendance at any hearing or inquiry at which he is a party or a witness, on notice, in company, not before eight in the morning. He is a **suspended man with a notebook, a cost and no authority**, and there is no mechanism in this country that can put those three words on a form in that order, and he has looked.

**Skill state. Stage 1 — Echo, and he has put it down.** **He read for the last time on Friday 27 November 2024, 10:45 to 10:54, in a public gallery, in front of eighty people, and he has said in a corridor, in a room with the door shut, and in writing on a card on a plinth: *I don't read anything again. Ever. Not the case, not the lamp, not a box in a cage, not a table in Ferry Bank, not a thing in a drawer in a laundrette.*** The reason is on the page and is not nobility: **as long as it is there it is the reason he gets in the rooms, and the rooms are the whole of what he does.** Four readings in the volume: a lamp, a loading bay, a sealed box, a public gallery, and this one. **He got the count, the record and the date, and nothing else, and the object did not appear and did not bless anybody and did not say who it was.** He is now, in Mara Iles's word, *a man in a profession that does not exist*, and the thing he can do is in a form posted to Northglass with a period of twelve months in the box for a period, and it is unanswered.

**The flaw, on the page, and it has stopped being a habit and started being a thing he is not going to do.** For six weeks it was *the only place a fact lives*. Then *the man in the room instead of the man with the fact*. Then *a person who causes the events he investigates should not be the investigator*. And now, in the eleven minutes he got in a director's office on the twenty-fifth of November: **it is not me that's the problem. It is the investigating.** He works it out in nine seconds in a corridor: every single thing that has happened to the families in this case got through a piece of paper and a person who is competent and who did not have to be brave, and **if one competent person moves about four inches to the left of her own department the whole building changes**, and the only reason this building is in the state it is in is that it is full of competent people who have all said *that is not my department*. And the new and final form of it, which is on the page on 29 November at 21:50 and is the most accurate description of him in the book: **nobody looked in his coat.** Nobody has ever known there was anything to look at. A suspended man is not a leaver and a leaver's things are searched, and there is no form for it, **and that is his punishment and it is his reward and they are the same object.**

**The nine times he did not ask.** The field officer, for a name for the two · Hattie, about the two in her room · Dael, about the figure · Mara, about the form · Hattie, for a count · **Doreen Sarn, for the address, until Thursday 12 November** · **Hattie Bramm, again, after she had handed the only sentence in the case to somebody else** · **number 14 Sillick Lane, on the evening of Saturday 21 November — the first one that was not a question to a person** · **the two women in the third row with a girl between them, on a pavement in Ferry Bank on the night of Thursday 26 November — the ninth, and Doreen Sarn said *good*, and it is the only one of the nine that cost nothing and was the right decision and he got no credit for it from anybody.** **The tenth is a knock on a door at number 88 in Sillick Lane at about ten to twelve on 13 December, and it is the last line of Volume 01.**

**What he has, at noon on 13 December 2024.** A pocket notebook, a hundred and fifteen pages, **a hundred and fourteen of them in a Museum file in a plastic sleeve that he does not have back and will not see again** · a cardboard folder on a shelf between a boiler and a chair with a company's letter in it and no Museum number on it · a copy shop wallet · **no card, no key, no radio, no torch, no post, and a pound** · a registered form with eleven stamps on it and an acknowledgment of delivery · a registered letter that has been in a coat pocket for nineteen days and is not his to open, which is Doreen Sarn's and which he has never read · a woman in Ferry Bank's sealed envelope opening at four o'clock today, of which he knows nothing.

**Costs unpaid and still owed.** **THE CHAPTER 10 LIE IS NO LONGER UNPAID. He named it himself, in a hearing in Ferry Bank on Thursday 26 November, in a statement read into a record that Field Officer Marsh undertakes will be read out in full to anybody who asks for a copy: the transcript clerk wrote *none produced* in a box on a pro forma and initialled it, she did not know she was writing a false thing, and he did not tell her, and it cost nothing and was not found. Mara Iles was in the second row. It is still not repaired by an apology; it is repaired by disclosure, in public, in a document, by the man who did it, and nothing further is owed on it and no later batch may restage it as a confession.** The Chapter 16 street. The bell at 11:52, which he has never told anybody, to anybody, in this volume. **Four seconds of a name, in October, which he cannot identify and never will.** **And fifty-seven minutes of his own name, in November, which cost him nothing and which he paid in a public gallery because a woman of fifty-four said it for him — reversible, not a memory cost, not displacement, and RULED: Volume 02 may not escalate it without recording the decision first.**

**What he is, on the record, at the end of the volume.** **He is on the payroll.** Ninth entry in the fixed-positions file of 16 September 2024, hours administered by the municipal bureau at cost centre M.Q.S. 4, remittance continuous and never stopped, and the Museum has been sending it. What he lost on 11 October was the right to be instructed. He said so himself, in full, to a room of thirty-one people on 26 November, and no chapter of this volume contradicts it anywhere.

**What he is frightened of, specifically.** Not the object, not the number, and not the post. **That in four years a person in a room will read line four of a minute — *the Museum has not investigated the conduct of any person* — and that will be the correct answer, and it will protect everybody including him, and it will mean that nobody is ever found out, and he will have to decide whether that is a good thing, and he already thinks it is not, and he has no standing whatever to think it.** And, second: **that the twenty-eight days have been spent on a question he wrote on the twelfth of December and told one person about on the same morning, and that the one person was the right one and that he chose her, and that a man who has learned to tell one person on purpose has still only told one person.**

## Mara Iles, 28

**What she has done in this batch, in order, and every item of it is a document.** She **built a one-page form for a man on a cleaning contract, left the grade box empty on purpose, and put the emptiness in the file**, and refused to sign it as an officer *because I am not going to be an officer on a piece of paper that puts a man in a bonded warehouse at eight in the morning.* She **fetched the 2006 file herself** and read a work authorisation and a pencil on a plan and **did not ask whether it was his father's.** She **disclosed a page of a contractor's day book into a Museum file by hand**, on a form with eight boxes and no number, and put into box five the sentence the volume is built on: **because nobody had asked me.** She **wrote the first entry since 11 September into the schedule of collections** and it is about something that is not the Museum's. She **identified the false payroll page as a forgery out loud in a public room** in two minutes, on two dates and an absence, and said she is not going to say who typed it and that the accession register's column is empty *and it is empty in the register and not in my imagination*. She **ruled at 09:00 on the Friday that column headings of a pay-office book are figures about things and not about people**, and read nineteen minutes of a wage book out loud in a public gallery, name by name, and did not say a total. She **refused him the word** and gave the reason. She **read a hundred and fourteen pages in twenty-two minutes without asking a question** and made no fuss, and wrote a minute, and did not give them back. She **wrote the initial on the restoration of a porter's name.** And she **left a message on a board about a door at 12:04 saying *not reported as an incident* and *it is written in the margin of the access printout in my file*.**

**Her five positions, all standing, and there is no reversal of any of them in this volume:** she will not be his witness · she will not open a box in a room with a camera in it · she will not take a number out of a notebook even if it is given to her · she will not be helped to ask the Registry's question · **and, new in this batch, she will not tell him what is on a card that is in a public room and not a Museum file.** *The reason is not discretion and it is not a protocol: the moment a person in a kitchen in Low Ward knows that a bell in a museum can be asked a question, that person will go and ask it… and there is no form in this country that can hold a chain of eleven good intentions, and the only thing that has ever held anything in this business is a record with a name against it, and you cannot get a name against that.*

**What she said to him in a conservation office with the door shut on 27 November, and has not unsaid and is the sentence Volume 02 inherits: *I do not know what you are for. That is not a figure of speech and I am not being kind. In no case has the reading been the answer to a question, and in every case it has been a thing that happened in a room while a man was standing in it, and I have spent six weeks waiting for a form that says what the room is supposed to do. And there isn't one. And you are going to spend the next eleven months looking for somebody to be in a room with you, and there is not going to be one, and the not-going-to-be-one is a policy, and the policy is being written by a woman of thirty-four in Northglass who has never seen a piece of the evidence and has been right about everything.***

## Ivo Pell, 25

**What he has done in this batch, and it is the smallest set of acts in the volume and the most important.** He **stood in the north door of the Hall of Former Faiths for two and a half months and again on the Friday morning of the memorial**, and the north door was the exit, and the Chair of the sub-group was told about it and said *he is not to be on the fire assembly point* and he said *fine, then I'll stand in the door*, and it was acceptable. **His paragraph on the back of the card is what makes the practice abandonable**, and on the Friday morning it is on a plinth in a plastic sleeve, and nobody in that room knew who had written it or when. **On the Friday morning of the memorial he is given a beat of his own in the preparation chapter, which the batch did not have and the review pass put in: he stands in the north door with a plywood board of Hattie Bramm's eighty names on it, a jug of water and eleven cups nobody asked for, and says out loud in a corridor that he will not be all right about saying it and that if he reads the back of the card this morning then that is the end of it and not a drill, and that nobody will get a second session out of him or out of anybody else in that building. And that the list is Hattie's, that he has not read one of the eighty names and is not going to, and that condition four is a limit and not a test, and that he has written that under the tape in marker pen where the queue can see it.** He is twenty-five and he is a systems technician and he is not a mystic and he is the only person in the building who can stop Elias Venn. He **put two lines in a conservation office on 27 November that are not his wife's and are his own** — *no person may require another person to continue; no person may ask this room a question about what is happening* — **and Mara Iles copied them onto a pad and took the credit, and told him why, and he did not enjoy it and she did not care.** He **put *Annexe D (permanent) is unaffected* on the closing notice of the Hall** because the Director made him, and he put nothing else on it. And he **carried the case cover to the Hall on the Friday morning and did not put it on the case**, and the reason it is not on the case is his and it has been the reason since the twenty-fourth of October.

**His condition from 1 December is in force and it bites in this batch**: every photographic exposure of collections material by a member of staff must be countersigned at the point of making, with the reason and the schedule reference. **In Chapter 50 it is on the reason he does not touch a postman's handheld**, along with the fact that the device policy in that building governs collections material and that he was four miles away and is still not going to be the reason a screen gets looked at twice. He is still a systems technician, still Saturday systems days, and he still has a sister with a chest. **He has not been thanked for the paragraph and the volume is not going to thank him.**

## Dael Ordway, 44

**What happened to him, in order.** He **wrote a one-page site risk assessment on Monday 23 November with four hazards on it before he knew three of them**, and line three is a weighbridge hump, and he read it out in a car and refused to cross it out, and he said why: *a form with the line on it is the only thing in this business that is going to keep me in a job, and I have known that since about half past one in the morning.* He **put his own name in a gate book in Low Ward at about twenty past eleven at night on a site he had no business on**, and asked for a man of fifty-four with a pen for a fence to be entered in the same line, and made him read it back. He **went over a weighbridge hump in the dark in heavy rain at about five to one on Tuesday 24 November and fractured the left clavicle, mid-shaft, displaced**, and the X-ray report says *following a fall from standing height onto a wet surface in an outdoor compound*, and **nobody is to blame.** He **asked, on a trolley, in an outpatients' corridor at half past one in the morning, whether the site had anybody on the sheet who knew he was there**, and when he was told the time off was blank, said *then write it now, while it's in your head* — and it is in the book, initialled, at a quarter to one in the morning, and that instruction is the reason he is not finished.

He **was removed from night command at 16:00 on Tuesday 1 December by nine minutes of a minute**, and the minute says it is not a finding that he falsified anything, and a trustee of sixty-four wrote the sentence that saved him after four minutes of silence from a director. He **came to the inquiry on 4 December on a crutch with a plastic folder and was a witness for two hours and ten minutes**, and he **gave them the true thing about Elias Venn, word for word, having said it first to a transfer clerk in a loading bay on 13 November and having never said it to him**, and he asked for that to be in the minutes. **Nobody in the room asked Dael Ordway a question about Elias Venn. There was not one, and the absence of a question is legible on a page.** He **was invited and not directed to produce any document, and produced the two that mattered unasked, in his own working capacity, and asked for that to be minuted in those words** — a phrase he has heard somebody else use in a room in October and has never mentioned that he has heard it.

**He is not an ally. He said so in a car at ten to four in the morning, at length, in his own words, and the sentence that carries it is: *I do not like what you have done to my gallery. I do not think the object on plinth thirty-four is a bell, and I have never said so to you and I am not going to start at half past four in the morning on a wet road with a broken collarbone. And I would put my name in a gate book beside yours any night of the week, and I could not tell you to your face why those two things are both true.*** He is a cautious witness in the inquiry. That is all he is going to be.

## Anja Ward, 53

**What she did in this batch, in order, and all of it is in a document and none of it is soft.** She **signed a form with a tick in a counter-signature box that says a person who is not an officer or employee of this Museum**, and she did it herself and she said out loud that she has never in nineteen years been an officer or an employee, and that if anybody asks in two years the answer is yes and her name is on it and it will be in a solicitor's file in Northglass in a fortnight. She **wrote twenty-eight load-bearing words into box three of the same form** and said that in nineteen years she has never wanted a form to say less. She **permitted the memorial and attached nine conditions, and said the price out loud in one sentence in a Board room**, and the ninth condition is the price: the Museum's own 1974 acquisition record on a table in a public room, in front of the people. She **let a trustee say that two of her conditions are not conditions, in front of a registrar, and answered *yes*.** She **refused a release from about seventy people who were short-paid for thirty-one years, in three sentences, and the reason is the whole of her character in the volume: *in four years somebody will stand up in a room and say the Museum got them to sign something, and it will be true, and there will be a document, and I would like it recorded that I did not dress it up in there and that nine sentences took me about twenty minutes and four of them were about what it costs them.*** She **signed the disclosure form six days late on Friday the twentieth and wrote two lines saying so and refused to have the date taken off the request.** She **put a registrar on the fourth floor at half past ten on the morning of the memorial for a reason that was five fifths true.** She **stood up against a wall at 10:37 on 27 November with her hands behind her back and told about seventy people that this Museum bought that mill office for nothing**, and then sat down against the wall. She **removed a man from night command and did not say one word about it.** And she **opened the door at 09:50 on 13 December with a registrar and a conservator and one person she has never met, and does not minute herself, and has not told him.**

**Her one sentence that is not procedure, in a corridor, at ten to five on 24 November:** *nineteen years of four o'clock meetings and a collections schedule and a rope line and a form for everything, and I have been perfectly correct every single day, and in nineteen years I have never once in this building opened a door for anybody… and on Friday morning at ten o'clock one of them is going to be opened by me, in public, with a minute about it, and I am going to be extremely unpopular with about nine people in this city, and four of them are on the Board and one of them is me. I would rather be hated than wrong.*

**What she still has not done.** She has still not told the Board about clause six. **She has no idea the twenty-eight-day question exists and never will in this volume.** She has not read the historical staff file. **She has now been told, by the inquiry, on the record, in her own words, that her eighteen-year assumption about the missing signature was wrong and that she built it on purpose in a corridor in October in about nine seconds to stop a director from opening a file.**

## Doreen Sarn, 78

**What she has done in this batch.** She **telephoned a bonded goods clerk at ten past five on Monday morning** and asked whether she could have until Friday to see whether anybody wanted to kill her, and was told no, and **telephoned back at nine the next morning, said the clerk was wrong about that, and said why, and the clerk wrote it down.** She **has now added four more refusals to a list of five and none of the five has moved**: she will not receive the book at the flat, in the hall or in a corridor · she will not sign anything, including the receipt · she will not read the book out to a camera · she will not be a witness, again, in a room with a form in it · **and she named the person who will sign the receipt, which is Hattie Bramm, *because she has got standing and I have got a memory, and a person with a memory is a person who can be disputed, and I have been disputed for fifty years and I am tired of winning.***

**And the instruction, which is the most important thing she says in the volume:** *you are not going to put the book in a case. You are not going to put it on a plinth with a rope line and a card and a pound a head. You are going to put it on a trestle table in the middle of that floor and you are going to let about seventy people walk round it and touch it if they want to, because it is a wage book and not a relic… and if the first thing that happens to it in a public room in this city is that it goes behind glass with a card on it then we will have spent fifty years and one broken collarbone to put a lie on a table.*

**And she told him to write down the moment he wants to be in the room on Friday afternoon** — *because if that man had been in the room then the first account of what was in it would have been his, and it would be true, and it would be a document with a Museum in it.* She was right and he wrote it down. **The box was opened in her own front room at four o'clock on Friday the twenty-seventh with two conciliation officers and a supervisor and a camera and no member of the Museum's staff in it, and it was not opened on a table by a man from a museum, and the first account of what was in it was hers.** **And on Sunday the sixth of December she opened the Museum's registered letter alone at her own table with the gas fire on, read it for about eleven minutes, and put it face down again, and nobody knows what is in it, including Elias Venn, who carried it for nineteen days and was told not to read it and has never asked.**

## Hattie Bramm, 54

**She has the room, and she did not ask for it, and the volume is able to prove that on the face of a document in three places.** She wrote to the Board on **Friday the twentieth of November**, in two pages, through the front desk, in an ordinary envelope with nothing on it, and the log line is in a clerk's hand, and the third paragraph says the count is to be called aloud **by her**, and the sixth says *I am asking for the list of who is in the room. And I am asking that the Museum does not decide it.* **She did that before there were conditions, before there was a date, and before Elias Venn knew anything, and he read it on the Tuesday in a Board packet and said one word about it in a loading bay and it was *wonderful* and it was not a question.**

**What she has refused in this batch, and all of it stands:** the list of eighty is hers and is not given to the Museum, done in pencil on the door, and *if you come over the number I'll tell you to go out* · the hundred and forty is not in the Museum and not in the conciliation service · **the three typed names go into Field Officer Marsh's file and not the Museum's, and the reason is that the second it is a thing that happened, one of the three is somebody's cousin.** She is a **member of the public and not a witness** at the hearing, and she said the thing about the people who never had a letter from the back, in her own voice, unbidden, and it is a file and not a gallery, which is what she wanted and which is the thing she has been refusing for nine years.

**What she did that nobody else could do.** She **paid the hundred and forty pounds at the door in notes and would not let anybody else pay it.** She **designed the panel and would not let anybody else do it**, and when a worker's name was proposed off the collection line she said *don't… a receipt's a document, a name under a receipt is a saint.* And **at about ten to eleven on the morning of the twenty-seventh, in a public gallery, with a registrar holding a form and a box that wanted a name in it and four seconds of silence, she said a stranger's surname out loud, and then said his name, and then said Ivy Case's, and told the room what he was.** She was asked to do nothing and she did it, and nobody in that hall knew, and she has not mentioned it since, and Elias Venn has written down that two people know and one of them has not mentioned it.

**She has still not been asked one question by him. Nine times. And on 13 December he goes up Sillick Lane past number 14, where she has a hundred and forty names in a drawer, and knocks at number 88.**

## Perry Loach, 61 (prose: *a night cleaner in his early sixties*)

**What he is in this volume, and it is the whole of the argument of the volume in one man.** He **says no to Elias Venn in a doorway on Tuesday the twenty-fourth of November**, and gives the reason: *I said no because it was Tuesday and you were in a doorway and it was a favour. That's all that was in it. There's no dignity in it.* And then the sentence that makes him the moral centre of the last movement: **I thought: they've had to make a piece of paper to give me a job they could have given me in a corridor in October. And that's a daft thing to think, and I thought it anyway, and it's true, and it's not about you. You never asked me for anything, not once, in six weeks, and I've been a floor in a corridor for six weeks wondering when you'd want something, and everybody wants something. So don't put your face like that.**

And what he asks for: **a piece of paper with his name on it, a time, what it is, who asked him, and a box at the bottom where he can write *agreed* or *not agreed*** — *and if I don't have a piece of paper then in about two years somebody's going to say *Loach was there*, and I'm going to have to say *was I?*, and that's the end of me.* **Mara Iles builds it in four minutes and leaves the grade box empty on purpose and puts the emptiness in the file, and he initials *agreed* at 11:04 on the twenty-sixth of November.**

**He is the reason a bonded book left a bonded store.** **Released to carrier P. LOACH, no employer of record.** He ties it in a plastic bag, then takes the bag out of the string and uses the new strap instead, because the strap was on it and the strap was Miss Arkwright's. He takes the 06:14 out of Northglass on his own. He waits in a public lobby on a bench for the loading bay and will not let Elias Venn come in with him. He puts it on a trestle and steps back four feet and looks at it for a minute and a half and says: *it's not a museum object. It's a wage book… and it has been on about four surfaces in eight weeks and every single one of them had a reason.* He signs his own form at the form's own box and does not want to be two minutes late for a grid that has his hours on it and no name on it. And on the Friday morning of the memorial **he is forty feet from an easel with a 1974 collection line on it and he does not move, and nobody in the room reads anything into it, and the chapter says so and does not say why.** He is not a saint and he is not on a panel and nobody has said he is related to anybody, **and the fifty-second line of a public schedule of interim payments says LOACH, P., Unclaimed, Held pending, and nobody in this building wrote it and nobody connected it.**

**He was asked one question, once, in six weeks, and it was not asked by anybody until Elias Venn asked it, in an aisle that is not where you stand, in a room of thirty-one people on the evening of 26 November: *what did they say to you, on that last morning, in the pay office, in the words they used to you? Not what happened. What they said.*** He said *I've got the words* and did not say them, and asked for it to be minuted that he was asked, and by whom, and at what time, and by a man who is not on the payroll, and that he will write it out and hand it in at the front desk in the morning, **and that it will be his words and not anybody's, and that if it's in a file it'll be because he put it there.** **What he wrote is not in this volume.** The numeral sixty-one does not appear in the same paragraph as his name anywhere in Batch 0005.

## Cliff Sowerby, 74, of 14 Sillick Lane

**Told, at last, at half past ten on Saturday the twenty-eighth of November, by the person who had carried his post for four weeks.** The letter is **the Museum's own printed card, dated Wednesday the nineteenth of November, sent to about four hundred addresses because the Hall of Former Faiths has a threshold group and Low Ward is in it and the Museum had never once sent him anything.** Hattie Bramm took it in through a box in his own front door on the thirtieth of October and put it in a drawer with the hundred and forty names *because I could not put a card about a bell in a drawer with a hundred and forty names because of what it would have been*, and **did not open it, because it was not addressed to her and she is not a person who opens other people's post.** He read it twice and asked one question: **is it the one off the poster?** He is on a list for 13 December, and Hattie Bramm is taking him on the tram, and *there are four hundred names on the Hall's list and mine is a different one and the two of them are going to be in the same room in about a fortnight.* **He is in the Hall on 13 December, on a bench against the west wall, with a folded newspaper on his knee, and he is in the room when the bell gives its note, and Elias Venn sees him from a pavement ninety feet away.**

## Aurelia Roth, 34, and Ottilie Grange, Registry assessors

**Roth is off-page in this batch and is the engine of it.** Her determination of 19 November, on the public register from the twentieth with a registration number, **is the object in every room Elias Venn enters in the next ten days.** Her sentence about the *whoever is in the room* mechanism is the argument a trustee puts to a Director in Chapter 43. **Her twenty-eight days produced a form, and the form was used on 12 December, and it is unanswered.** Her refusal to help him frame the question produced the only version of it that could have been written. And her prediction, which Mara Iles repeats to him on a telephone on 12 December: *it will be listed, and it will come to a day with a name on it, and you will get a letter, and it will be from a woman who has never seen a piece of the evidence, and she will be right, and it will take about eleven weeks.* **Grange does not appear.** **A third assessor, of about fifty-five, on the rota, certifies the Gallery of Unnamed Hands on 24 November and refuses to write the word *unauthorised* anywhere.**

## H. Lound — Chief Clerk, Remittances, Municipal Payroll Bureau

**Called twice, and paid twice.** **MPB/R/8841** of 29 October, and now **MPB/R/9016 of 24 November 2024, in answer to MPB/R/8841**, and it is in the hearing bundle of 26 November and it is four of the four documents behind the restoration of a porter's name. Q1, did this office pay this person: **YES, remittances continuous to date, at the deductions recorded, at cost centre M.Q.S. 4.** Q2, was this person employed by anybody: **No response available from this office. This office holds a record of a person and of money paid. It does not hold a record of employers.** **Field Officer Marsh read the form into the record twice, once as written and once with the second question spoken aloud, because the second question is in the file and the answer to it is not, and both of those are facts and only one of them is a thing a person has to answer for.** He has never heard of the building. He is furniture and he is the most useful piece of furniture in the volume, and he has now been asked the same question twice and the second time one half of the answer was nothing.

## Miss Arkwright — bonded goods clerk, Broomhead Reach

**She is the volume's argument in a person and she has the best lines in the last movement.** She is competent, courteous, six years in the sheds, and completely blameless, and she raised a proper hold on her own system on a letter off a Director in ninety seconds, and then said to two people, before she would do anything else: **there is no indemnity in this, and I have not asked for one, because this store does not sell the goods and does not own the goods and does not care what the goods are. This store sells a guarantee that nothing leaves without a piece of paper.** And the sentence that closes her argument: **that is the whole of bonded. Everything else is just a warehouse with a fence round it.** She also said, on the Monday morning, the thing nobody else in the volume can say: **on Friday the thirteenth a registrar could not describe her own collection, and on Monday the twenty-third a night officer with a broken man in the back of the car got a description out of a weighbridge hut, and the two facts are nine days apart and they are the same fact, and it is the only thing I have learned in six years that I did not know in the first one.** And she is the one who says that a person with no employer of record is the easiest release she has ever done, *because there is nothing for me to prove.*

## Arthur Neve, 78 — Ravenscourt and Hale, 6 Northgate Steps

**He produced the buyer's carbon on Thursday 26 November at 18:00, and he had said three times that it was his document until the day.** He came up the stairs at ten to six, sat at the front because he asked to be at the front, put the carrier bag on the table, and did not take the carbon out, and read it out himself, and said *I am going to read it out myself, and if I cannot read a line then I am going to say *I cannot read this line* and I am not going to be helped.* He read the three differences and the figure and *received, all, subject to weight*, and then said: **the clerk has certified the comparison and she says she does not certify that either document is correct, which is the first sentence anybody has said to me since October that I did not expect.** The carbon went into the bundle on a conciliation service document. It has left his hands once, in a room, on the twenty-sixth, and that is the first time in fifty years it has been in anybody else's.

## Margaret Priest, about fifty, front desk, nineteen years

**Read a day book out loud to a man standing on the other side of it, because he asked her to, and then wrote the only line in that book in nineteen years that does not contain a name.** *11:06 — [LINE ENTERED BY COUNTER STAFF AT SUBJECT'S REQUEST.] … Name not spoken and not entered by subject. Counter staff cannot certify identity. Not to be queried.* And then, to nobody: *that's the only time I have ever written that in that book.* And earlier, to a man who said it was the best thing anybody had said to him in six weeks: **it's not a thing. It's a Tuesday.**

## Mrs Farrier, 58, key board, security room, fourteen years

**Never asked anybody why in fourteen years, and told the only person who ever has.** *There's not one for Case either. I don't know that from a file, love. I know it because I've been on this board fourteen years and the ones with leaver's cards have leaver's cards and the ones without don't, and there have been about nine in fourteen years and I could not tell you a single one of them what they were, and I have never once asked, because it isn't the board's job and I'm on the board.* **And she is the reason nobody looked in his coat, because a suspended man is not a leaver and a leaver's things are searched and there is no form for that.**

## Relationships, end of Volume 01

- **Elias / Mara:** an arrangement that has become a practice. **She has his hundred and fourteen pages and a receipt on the back of a page and the initial on a restored name and the only sentence anybody has said to him that is a policy rather than a feeling — *I do not know what you are for* — and he has a hundred and fifteen pages in a coat with nothing left in them that is about him.** She is the only person who has ever refused him something he could have taken, and he is the only person she has ever refused to be a witness for twice. **There is no romance and there is no confession and the volume ends with both of them in separate rooms, four miles apart, on a Saturday morning, with a piece of paper between them.**
- **Elias / Ivo:** a working friendship in which a man of twenty-five gave away the only sentence in the case and a man of thirty-two has spent seven weeks not asking him for it, and the last movement is built entirely out of a paragraph a systems technician wrote for nobody in a loading bay on 24 October and put on a plinth. **Neither of them has said the word *thank you* and it is not going to be said and it does not need to be.**
- **Elias / Dael:** **mutual declined, and it is now four times, and it is a structure and not a failure.** A night supervisor takes a suspended night guard to a site at night because it is the only lawful door and it is his only authority, and a man goes over a hump and breaks a collarbone, and in a car at ten to four in the morning he says *I am not your friend* and means it, and eleven weeks later he gives the sub-group the true thing about the man he will not befriend, and the two of them have still not discussed it. **The most functional relationship in the building.**
- **Elias / Hattie:** **a debt paid nine times and not paid, and the tenth entry is a knock on a door in her street.** She said his name for him in front of eighty people and has not mentioned it. He has got her sentence off a man of twenty-five and given it away and worked out why and crossed most of it out. **He has never asked her one question and she has never asked him for anything and she is the only person in this business who has a form that can take a man's own words and will not let him near it.**
- **Elias / Doreen Sarn:** **the only relationship in the volume in which somebody has told him something he did not know, three times, and in which he gave something away that was not a document.** He told her a date and she believed him because nobody wanted to be in the room. He carried her registered letter for nineteen days and did not read it. She refused to be a record five times and named the person who would be. And on a pavement on 26 November she told him about two women in a third row and he did not ask, and she said *good*, and it is the only time in the volume that somebody has approved of him.
- **Elias / Anja:** **authorisation without information, then a decision with letters on it, then a card read out in a corridor, then a tick in a box, then a minute with four lines, and then a door.** She wrote twenty-eight load-bearing words so that he would not belong to her institution; she removed him from a building he was never in; she stood against a wall and said *we bought it for nothing*; and she opened a door he was not allowed to see. **They have not had a conversation in this volume that was not about a document, and there is not going to be one.**
- **Mara / Anja:** a clause in a margin, a Sunday in a corridor, a minute note under a Board paper, a review on a Wednesday, a form signed six days late, **and a form built by the registrar and given to her to sign at 11:04 on 24 November, and the Director signs it and learns on the Friday what is in box five.** Neither of them has mentioned any of it and both of them will.
- **Mara / Aurelia Roth:** a procedure that will not do what it was written to do, and a woman who was right about it three times, and a form that was used on the twelfth of December, and neither of them is on the other's side and neither of them is the other one's enemy. **Roth does not know he has posted it. She will find out in about eleven weeks, and Mara Iles has already told him what her letter will sound like.**
