# CHARACTER STATE — Volume 02, Batch 0001 (Chapters 51–60), as of Monday 11 January 2025, about 23:30

Read against `state/continuity.md`, which is the authority. **Ages are canon and unchanged: Elias Venn 32 · Mara Iles 28 · Ivo Pell 25 · Anja Ward 53 · Hattie Bramm 54 · Doreen Sarn 78 · Perry Loach *a night cleaner in his early sixties* · Cliff Sowerby 74 · Margaret Priest about fifty · Mrs Farrier 58 · Fenn surname only · Field Officer Marsh · Vera Pilling 81 · Marion Kelsh 54 · S. Tully about forty-five · Mrs Toft about fifty · Roy Tilley about fifty · R.M. · Denise Hargreaves · Sonia Boulton about thirty-nine · Frank Dunning about sixty-five.**

## Elias Venn, 32

**Where he is.** A rented room above a laundrette in Low Ward. No post, no access, no employer, no card, no key, no radio, no torch. Barred from the Museum as from 1 December 2024 as a security matter, save attendance at any hearing or inquiry at which he is a party or a witness, on notice, in company, not before eight in the morning — **and the inquiry is his only lawful door, and it is a witness notice under item six, and it is a card with a date and a time and the word WITNESS in a box, and he has been in a room four times in ten days and the last time was 22 December.** He has a **pound**, a **new pocket notebook bought on the corner for thirty-five pence with nine pages in it and eleven lines on the first of them**, a **cardboard folder on a shelf between a boiler and a chair** with a company's letter in it that he has not put in a Museum file and is not going to, on purpose, under clause six, a **municipal remittance that arrives on the first of the month at cost centre M.Q.S. 4 for a post that no longer exists**, and his name on a stair list at a letting agency, which is cash on a Friday and is not a job.

**Skill state. Stage 1 — Echo, and he has put it down, and he has not picked it up once in ten days and nobody has asked him to.** He has **not read anything.** The mat at 88 Sillick Lane is on the floor of a public gallery four inches long and he stood six feet from it in the Hall of Former Faiths on 22 December with his hands behind his back and did not come closer and did not look away, and **that is the hardest thing he has done since the twenty-seventh of November and nobody in that building will ever know it was hard.** The notebook is the only instrument he has, it is a record nobody has authorised, it is nine pages, and the volume's thesis is that a record with a name against it is the only instrument there is and his cannot carry his name.

**The cost curve is UNSPENT.** No second notebook entry of any kind. No memory cost, no time lost, no name gone, no blood, no headache. The Chapter 8 two-hands entry is untouched. **The fifty-seven minutes of 27 November are untouched and were not escalated.** The first major memory-displacement cost remains reserved for Volume 03. **The hundred and fifteen pages are a Museum record in a plastic sleeve in a drawer on the first floor and he does not have them back and is not going to.**

**The thing that changed and is not a cost. He has noticed, and has not admitted to himself in prose, that he has stopped counting objects and started counting rooms.** He counts the doors he has been behind, and whether each one had a form on it, and whether the form had a box for a person, and whether anybody's name was in the box. He did it in a lobby on 16 December and could not stop and was embarrassed and did it anyway and wrote it out. **And on 30 December, in a corridor, he noticed that everything that has ever happened to him in this business has happened on a Tuesday, and that this one happened on a Wednesday, and he did not write it down.**

**His eleven times of not asking, and the two that were not not-askings.** Nine were in Volume 01 and the tenth was the knock at 88 on Sunday 13 December. The **eleventh** was hers: *On the sheet. **Is your name still in the field at number fourteen.*** **The twelfth** was at a school: *You've got a line number — is it a number?* **And the thirteenth is not a question at all: *Can somebody write down what it is, then*** — asked by Frank Dunning in a lobby to a registrar, and it is the first time in this business that a person has asked him for nothing and been refused nothing because he was not the right man for it, and he stood six feet away and let it happen, and that is a growth and he does not have a word for it and nobody is going to give him one.

**His position, said out loud for the first time and it is not temporary.** Chapter 59: the practice is a record with a name against it, made by a person, at a time, in front of a witness — **and he cannot be the person who makes it, because a record with his name on it is a record about a man who is in a disciplinary file four miles away, and a woman of fifty-four has said out loud in a room of eighty people that a name in a Museum file is a thing that happens to a person, and she was right, and he has been on the wrong end of it for six weeks and knows exactly what it costs.** Chapter 60: *I am barred from the building and I am not a member of the public of anything and I have no standing in any room in this city, and the last time I was in a room with a form in it I was a witness, and the time before that I was a man with a notebook in a doorway, and before that I was a man in a corridor at twenty-six minutes past one in the morning with nothing.* **And: so somebody has to be asked, in a room, with a document. That is the whole of what I have learned, and I cannot do it, and a man of twenty-five has to write a sentence about a person by Wednesday.**

**What he is frightened of, specifically, and it has changed.** Not the post, and not the pound, and not the fifty-seven minutes. **That the only instrument in this business is a record with a name against it, and he has nine pages, and the only good copy of four hundred and twelve names is in a building he cannot enter, on a wire that is overwritten every morning and has no memory, and the Museum prints what it is given and has never read its own print run, and the person who lost a bell to one of his own conditions is now going to close a gallery for ten working days over a doormat, and everybody in the chain is doing a correct thing inside a department, and nobody in the chain has ever been asked what a name field is for.** And second: **that he told one person about the twenty-eight days and has never told the Director, and that a man of twenty-five is now going to write a sentence about a person to a stranger, and the sentence is the thing he cannot write, and it is being written by somebody else.**

**He still has not told anybody about the bell at 11:52 on 11 November 2024.** It is named once, in Chapter 60, in a set of figures he cannot prove, with the word *or* in front of his own figures as the honest part of the page, and there is a woman in Sillick Lane with a hundred and forty names in a drawer and a woman of seventy-eight who has read a sheet of carbon in her own hand and a man of twenty-five with a mug in his hand, and none of them knows, and he is not going to tell them, and there is nobody in this city he is allowed to tell.

**His stop word: he has none, he never had one, he did not learn one, and on 7 January 2025 Mara Iles said out loud that she is the person who would have to give him one and has decided not to.** It was not a kindness and not a punishment, and the reason is on the page: *a word that comes from a registrar in a building four miles away is not a word anybody in Sillick Lane can use at four o'clock in the afternoon when it is getting dark.* **The practice has one and it is a permission and it belongs to a street. Batch 0002 must not let it become his, and if anybody offers him one he refuses and the refusal is on the page.**

**The one gift he got in ten days and it was not a gift.** A sheet of Museum notepaper with no number on it, in the post on Tuesday 15 December, four days late, inviting him to a Sunday morning that had passed and to a door he could not have opened, because **a person who is not Museum staff and is not a member of the public is, by the terms of the exception in line three of the minute, a person with no lawful way in.** He is still the only person in the city who has ever been in the fourth level, and the room has not been described, and he does not know what happened in it and will be told in four words in a corridor by somebody who does not owe it to him, or not at all.

## Mara Iles, 28

**What she did in this batch, in order, and every item of it is a document or a refusal that is a document.** She **took four days to find no form for a household object and then found the right instrument in about four minutes because a systems technician said *there's a book*.** She **described a door mat in four minutes and eleven seconds with a steel rule, on Museum notepaper, in a public room, in the presence of the presenter, with no accession number assigned and none proposed** — and she **refused to make a card for it, on both grounds, and said that both of them were decisions and neither of them was a rule.** She **refused to leave a blank on a public card about a mat and said why: *I do not yet know what this building does with a card when it cannot be bothered to fill it in, and I have watched this building for five years and the answer is that it fills it in later.*** She **drafted a movement docket on Monday the twenty-third of December and watched the best condition in the building fail**, and her sentence is the finding: *the moment we make it one, in order to get it out of the building, we have built ourselves a rule that says he cannot have it back.* She **read the sub-group resolution of 18 November out in full, in a corridor in a school in Hall Lane, to a woman of fifty-four and a man of thirty-two, because it is a resolution about that room and it has never been read to a school.** She **read the answer off a sheet on 28 December and put it in the systems office with four lines and a distance on it and the Registrar's initials**, and the distance is on the sheet because somebody asked for it. She **took her reading glasses off in a room on 11 January, which she had not done in the room before, and gave the batch's answer: *For the bill. That is the answer and it is the right answer and it is the whole answer… and it is the most important sentence anybody has said in this building since the seventh of November, and it is not a sentence anybody said. It is a definition. It is in a box at the top of a form.*** She **refused to describe the unlisted room and did not need to, because nobody asked her and it is item 1 and it is done.** And she **told him on the telephone on 7 January that nobody was ever going to give him a stop word**, and gave the reason, and did not soften it.

**Her five positions, all standing, and there is no reversal of any of them in this batch:** she will not be his witness · she will not open a box in a room with a camera in it · she will not take a number out of a notebook even if it is given to her · she will not be helped to ask the Registry's question · she will not tell him what is on a card in a public room and not a Museum file. **And the sixth, new in this batch, and it is the same family and it is the whole of her:** *I have been trying to find a Museum instrument for three days and I have walked past the correct one, and it is a book with a ribbon in it and a woman at a counter, and I have been so busy finding the right form that I did not see the right one.*

## Ivo Pell, 25

**What he did in this batch, and it is the smallest set of acts in the volume and it is the engine of the last eight days.** He **said *there's a book*** in a loading bay on Christmas Eve and found the instrument that had defeated a registrar for three days, and the reason he found it is that he has never had to think about a document that is not a form. He **did four hundred and some dead letters on a Saturday because a suspended man was in the room, in forty minutes, and wrote the number and his own name on a new envelope for the first time since 8 December**, and left three charity items in the general pile because he is not a registrar and it is not his job to decide whose post a thing is. He **printed the threshold-group block and put it next to two print runs and found nine blanks in one run of the sheet, and wrote the comparison down by hand because a screen may not be photographed, and had it initialled by the Registrar, and his condition of 1 December is on the page and unengaged.** He **read the district's occupancy definition and discovered that the Museum is downstream of a delivery sheet and has never been told**, and said: *If I knew about clause seven since the second week I was here and I have never used it, because there was never anything to use it on.* He **did the arithmetic standing up and found that clause 7 closes on Wednesday the thirteenth of January and that nobody in the building has ever known that**, and he is the only person in the world who has ever counted, and he said so, and it was not a joke. He **has to write a sentence by Wednesday about a person, to a records officer who has never been asked for anything, and he has never written a sentence in that agreement in four years, and he said he would rather do four hundred envelopes than sit in a systems office with a mug, and he meant it.**

**His condition from 1 December is in force and it bites in this batch** and it is on the page twice: *If we photograph anything on that screen, my condition from the first of December kicks in and it needs your countersignature at the point of making and the reason and the schedule reference, and I have countersigned four exposures since the first of December, all of them mine, and I have not enjoyed one of them. So we are not photographing anything.* And it is honoured by not photographing, and the alternative is stated and it is better: *We write them out. On paper. In ink. And a registrar signs it, and it is a document, and it is not a photograph, and it can be read in a hearing in March by a man in a wig who will not be interested in whether the screen was photographed.*

**And he is the only person in the building who has ever said what a name field is for, and he gave the answer that the batch needed and then corrected the questioner: "For finding him. That's what it does. It doesn't know that… it's a lookup, and it will go and get you a postcode just as fast. It's not for the name."** And: *nobody knows. That is the actual answer. It was built for a bill, and then about forty other things got hung off it… and if you asked the man who wrote the original instruction in about 2011 what the field was for he would say a bill, and he would be right, and it would not help.* He is twenty-five, a systems technician, not a mystic, and **he has not been thanked for any part of it and the volume is not going to thank him.**

## Anja Ward, 53

**What she did in this batch, and it is all in a document and none of it is soft.** She **re-read a sentence she said in a corridor on 24 November and gave the reason for it, which is the reason the batch is stuck: *It is because I have nothing to be responsible for. I cannot be responsible for a thing that has no number, in a building whose every control is a number… and if I sign a piece of paper saying that I have it, then somebody in four years is going to come and ask me where it is, and I am going to have to say I signed a piece of paper about it in December 2024 and it did not have a number on it, and that is a sentence about me and not about a mat, and I have spent nineteen years not having a sentence about me in this building.*** So: no, for two days. She **signed the second document of December at about twenty past eleven on the morning of the thirtieth, and said the cost out loud before she signed and had it minuted in that order**, and the cost is a gallery closed for ten working days over the holiday, about ninety people at a shut door on a Friday, and about four hundred and twelve households who were told on the second of December that the room was open. And: **we're going to be about ninety people at that door on the Friday the eighth and I have said the cost out loud first, in a room, in December, on purpose, because I am not going to be a woman who says it in January.** And: **a door mat has more classification in front of it than a bell has, and I would take a mat over a bell every day of my working life and I have.** She **recorded the day of the week on the minute, because a condition that counts working days has to hang off a date somebody can check, and she is a Director who has been caught out by a weekday exactly once in nineteen years and does not intend to be caught twice.** And she **had a Registry assessment of about forty-five sitting in a file for eleven days without telling the sub-group, because she wanted the minute to have a cost in it.** And into a silence, about her own condition, for the fourth time in two months: **then I have written a condition that can hold a door mat in a building for ever and I could not have told you that in October and I have been a Director for nineteen years and I did not know, and I am not going to pretend I designed it and I am not going to pretend I did not.** And she **signed a condition-three closure of the Hall of Former Faiths for 31 December to 13 January which the Board saw in January and did not move, because the sub-group does not move a Director's date.**

**She is not an ally and she has not become one and she is not going to.** They have not had a conversation in this batch that was not about a document, and the one line that was in the visitors' book at the end of the visitors' book is the entire relationship: **A. WARD, Dir., asked.** — the only document in the business in which a Director of this Museum has recorded that she was asked to do a thing rather than that she did it, and she asked for it to be worded that way, and the person who got her to ask was a night cleaner in a corridor on Christmas Eve.

## Doreen Sarn, 78

**Unmoved, and it is on the page.** She **refused a sixth time, in a room, on 18 December, with two witnesses, through a woman who came down from Ferry Bank on a bus to deliver four words, and asked to have it written why: that it would be the sixth thing she has refused and none of the five before it moved either.** The reason is unchanged since October and it is four lines: **she will not be a record again, in a room, with a form in it.** She has refused to receive the book at the flat, in the hall or in a corridor; to sign anything including the receipt; to read the book out to a camera; to be a witness again; and now this. **None of the six has moved and she is right about all six and the bus fare is the cost and nobody itemises it.** She has not been in a room and she has not moved and nothing is owed that she has asked for.

## Hattie Bramm, 54

**She has the room and she has said no to him ten times in a row and then answered the eleventh, and the answer was two words long, and it is the most useful thing anybody has given him in six weeks.** She **would not have him in the back room at the library and gave him a rule as the reason: *there is no such room in Low Ward a person can be in without a form, and I have been doing this in a back room with a kettle for nine years.*** And: *if you come over the number I'll tell you to go out.* And the sentence that is the best thing in Chapter 53 and is not a line: **if you had come in here without a face on you I would have given you a chair and put you on the list, and the list is a piece of A4 and a pencil and it goes in a drawer, and I would have done it, and it would have been the best Thursday of my life.** She **sent Frank Dunning to a museum she had never been inside, to a woman at a counter she had never spoken to, and did it without being asked and cannot be instructed and has now done a thing for this Museum twice and been thanked for neither.** She **gave the batch its two sentences in a library back room, four times, to four different people, none of whom wrote down the same version, which is how he knows it was not a speech: *nobody is doing this to anybody* and *the post is the register*.** She **refused the hundred and forty and gave the reason out loud twice, and refused the three typed names a second time for the same reason, and said that a list of people is not evidence until it is in a file and the second it is in a file it can be used.** And she **told him where he got her sentence from, and made him say so, and did not let him have it back.** And she **answered the eleventh question of his life in four seconds: *Aye. It's got my name on it and it's had since about 1974 and I have never once wanted to read it.*** *Why not.* **"Because I'm the one who writes them."** **She is a member of the public and not a witness and she has never once been asked to be anything else, and her Friday list is twenty names in pencil and it is not kept and it is the people who turn up.**

## Perry Loach, *a night cleaner in his early sixties*

**He is the reason a door mat left a public museum, and it is canon and it is the whole of the argument in a man.** He **said no to Elias Venn a second time, in a service corridor at five past five on Christmas Eve, and the reason is the same shape as November and better: *You asked me. That's the whole of it and you didn't even notice you'd done it… if a thing of mine is on a piece of paper in a corridor in this building on Friday because a man without a job asked me, then on Friday somebody is going to look at that piece of paper and find my name on it attached to a door mat… You don't want a thing of mine to end up on a piece of paper because you asked. You want the office to ask. You want somebody to do something, you get a letterhead to do it with.*** And when told he cannot ask the office because he is barred from the building: **"Then you're stood up."** And he was asked by the Director's office, in person, and **said yes, and the reason it worked is four words in a corridor on Christmas Eve: Tell him the office asked.** He **walked a door mat out of the Museum of Quiet Saints at about twenty past five on 24 December 2024, down two flights, across a yard, out of a roller door and down to the gate, in nine minutes, in a plastic bag, on no docket**, and the record says: **Carried by P. LOACH, services contract. No grade exists. GRADE FIELD LEFT EMPTY AT THE COUNTER'S INSISTENCE.** **There is still no column in the contract return in which a person can be written, so the only person who can be named as a carrier on a Museum form and not be an officer or employee is a night cleaner on a contract.** He **was not thanked, and is not going to be, and the volume says so twice.** He is not a saint and he is not on a panel and nobody has said he is related to anybody, and the fifty-first line of a public schedule of interim payments says LOACH, P., Unclaimed, Held pending, and nobody has connected it to a man in overalls, and **Cliff Sowerby now knows that he is on that schedule too, and has not been told which line, and Elias Venn did not tell him and was not asked to.**

## Cliff Sowerby, 74, of 14 Sillick Lane

**Told everything, finally, except the thing he was owed.** He was told about a printed card on 28 November by a woman of fifty-four; he came to the reopening on 13 December and sat on a bench with a folded newspaper; **and on 8 January 2025 at about half past six, in a front room with a fire on, Elias Venn knocked and handed him a registered letter he had carried since 30 October and had never opened and had never been asked for.** *I'm not going to give you this in a corridor. I'm going to knock, and I'm going to stand there, and I'm going to hand it to you, and you're going to open it, and I'm going to watch you read it because I have to know that you read it and you have to know that I knew that you hadn't.* It is four lines from a firm of solicitors at 14 Lincoln's Lane and it says a schedule of interim payments has been opened in the matter of payments for the final trading week of October 1974, that a line has been opened in his name, that the line is public, and that he may come to the conciliation office on any weekday between ten and four and ask to see it. **"I have been on a list since the Thursday before I came to that gallery and nobody has said so, and I have got a stamp on my hand in biro from the thirtieth of October, and I have been carrying it round for two months."** *You could have brought it round in November.* **"No. You couldn't have. It wasn't yours and it wasn't a Tuesday."** He put it on the sideboard next to the teapot **and did not put it in a drawer.** He is seventy-four and he is on a public schedule and he has a place on a list for the reopening and he lives in a row whose name fields are going empty and **he is on the sheet, so the practice can reach him, and he is the reason one household in that row is not a moral floor.**

## Margaret Priest, front desk, nineteen years

**She gave a man his own card back across a counter and it is the only thing she has done in nineteen years that was not asked for.** *Because you came up this counter on Monday and asked me for it, and because I have been putting things in boxes on this counter for nineteen years and about four of them in nineteen years have ever come back out again, and I would like there to be one.* **She gave the two truths and refused to give only the comfortable one**, and the other one is the volume: *We don't make that list. We print off a file that comes down from the district on the first of the month… and the names on that card came into this building off a computer somewhere and I have never seen the computer and I have never asked.* And she **told him the number of the returned cards and would not soften the count**, and she **took a message from a school in Hall Lane about a mat in a corridor and put it on a board, which is what the board is for, and the Museum was shut and the message sat for four days over Christmas and nothing happened until the twenty-ninth, and that is the correct behaviour of a board and it is why the batch's escalation was a card and not a decision.** And she **wrote *not a Museum object* in a reason column and initialled it**, and the same hand, on the same day, wrote *A. WARD, Dir., asked* on the last line of a visitors' book, which she did without being asked and which nobody has explained and **she is the only person in this business who is the lawful door and does not know she is the instrument.**

## Fenn, surname only, label room

**She asked for the foot of the plinth-34 card to be read before she cut a new one, and the reading of it is the batch's turn.** *Either I put a name in it or I leave a hole. And I'm not putting a name in it. And I can't leave a hole, because everybody before me made sure you can't leave a hole. And the way they made sure of it is the thing I want somebody to look at before I do it.* — *Eleven feet that way. It's the one that's been under a panel for a month.* And then, on being asked what happens if it stays blank: **"Then it stays blank, and it is the first one in a hundred and forty years, and every person who comes in here for the next hundred and forty years will look at it and see a space where the name should be, and they will not know that anybody decided anything. And you know what a space is, don't you. A space is the most honest thing a card has ever had on it and it is the single most dangerous thing you can put on a public card in a museum, and both of those are true, and I am going to do it, and I am not going to be thanked for it, and I would like it minuted that I said it was dangerous first."** **She has made about four hundred cards and has never once been asked whether the name on one of them is right, and she has a line in the plinth 34 card in her own field proving why.** Surname only, always.

## The new working people of Batch 0001, all competent, none a villain, each of them the argument in a person

- **Frank Dunning**, about sixty-five, **88 Sillick Lane**. *I've never had a letter. I've had a bin bag and a bank.* Names the practice, keeps the sheet on the table in his hall, and says the floor: *the ones on the sheet we can get to, the ones off it we've not got a door.* **He is the one person in this batch who is not frightened and is not reassured and is not distressed, and the reason is that nobody has ever asked him a question about it.**
- **Roy Tilley**, about fifty, postman, **Fisher Lane and Sillick, FLS-2**, about two hundred and ten addresses, one man because the other is long-term sick. **He is the only witness anybody has**, he signed on Sunday the thirteenth that a name field doesn't go empty by accident, he believes the sheet, and he is going to be moved to another round in February.
- **R.M.**, a counter clerk of about twenty-seven at the delivery office. **The first instrument anybody has handed anybody in this batch, and the first time in eleven weeks that somebody has handed him anything.** *If I put it in a bin bag in this street then in a fortnight there's a man at a hearing with a printer's copy of an extract of a field operations log and no idea where it came from, and that's not a piece of paper, that's a weapon with no name on it.* And: *You've been the fourth one this week and you're the first one who's read the code legend.*
- **Denise Hargreaves**, delivery office supervisor. **The depot's position, given twice, correctly: the sheet's right.** And the sentence the whole of the batch is built on, from a woman in her twenties who has been in the job nine years: **it doesn't ask whether the house has gone, it asks whether we've got somebody to give it to, and those are not the same and that is the entire problem and it has been the entire problem since about 1994 and she has never once been able to say it to anybody who wanted to hear it.**
- **Marion Kelsh**, 54, school business manager, **St Anselm's (Hall Lane)**, nineteen years. **Stopped it in nine minutes with a school form and a scale rule and her own telephone, and does not know that a condition dated the first of December exists anywhere in this city.** And: *Your museum has a card with a name on it, and my school has a book with a name on it, and we are the only two places in this county that could put a name on that mat. And the school isn't going to, because she's nine.* And the sentence about the warm place by the radiator, and the girl who stands on it every morning, and the thirty-eight children in the room who do.
- **Vera Pilling**, 81, of **6 Sillick Lane**. **The first person to try it, and it cost her her chest.** Said a dead man's name at a living man's door and got *Who?* and corrected the practice herself: *you can't just say a name, you've to knock first.* **The mouth, not the god: *I have not been called anything in this street for nine years, not by post and not by mouth.***
- **S. Tully**, about forty-five, **Registry assessor**, the first of a name on a rota to be said out loud in this building, who refused the Museum and told it not to display the object and said *I am not sorry*, and who has never been spoken to.
- **Mrs Toft**, about fifty, **records officer, district addressing service, Marsh Lane**, six years in post, keeper of a supplied-file archive **nobody has ever asked her for**, who said *that is not a complaint, I am telling you it because you asked me the question as though it was ordinary and it is not ordinary.* And who has never been asked for anything at all, and who is going to be telephoned on Wednesday.
- **Sonia Boulton**, about thirty-nine, letting agent, Sillick Lane, with a book. *That's not an employer, love. That's a place the money goes through.* And: *a person who says I was good at doors and then stops is a person who is telling you the truth about the size of it.*
- **Field Officer Marsh**, who came to a Friday queue in a back room and said two things out loud before anybody spoke: *what is said in this room is said in a room, it is not a claim and it is not a record, and it goes nowhere* — and *I write down what other people have already done, with the name of the person who did it on it. That is the whole of my trade. There is no version of me in this room today.*

## Relationships, end of Volume 02 Batch 0001

- **Elias / Mara:** an arrangement that has become a practice and a policy she has stated out loud. **She telephoned on 7 January to tell him he will never have a stop word and gave the reason and it was not a kindness.** He has not asked her for anything in ten days and has not withheld anything except the bell. **There is no romance and no confession and the ten days end with him in a rented room and her on the first floor of a building he cannot enter, with a piece of paper between them and a clause seven in it.** And *a word that comes from a registrar in a building four miles away is not a word anybody in Sillick Lane can use.*
- **Elias / Ivo:** a working friendship in which a man of twenty-five has said the batch's answer twice, corrected the questioner, and is now the person who has to write a sentence about a person by Wednesday because a man with a notebook cannot get into the building. **Neither of them has said the word thank you and it is not going to be said and it does not need to be.**
- **Elias / Hattie:** a debt paid ten times and not paid, and the eleventh answer, and **it was two words long and it was the most useful thing anybody has given him in six weeks.** She has still never been asked for anything. He went past her house on 13 December and knocked at 88 instead and she watched him and said nothing and has now sent a man to a museum at fourteen doors away because she decided to.
- **Elias / Frank Dunning:** the one relationship in this batch that begins with a knock and turns, in ten days, into a working arrangement between two people who cannot help each other institutionally. **He is the only person in this business who has handed Elias Venn a sentence he cannot use and told him the reason, and the sentence is what this case is about.**
- **Elias / Anja:** authorisation without information, then a decision with letters on it, then a card read out in a corridor, then a tick in a box, then a minute with four lines, then a door, and now **a line in a visitors' book with the word *asked* at the end of it, put there because a night cleaner told a man with no post to get a letterhead.** They have not had a conversation in this batch that was not about a document.
- **Elias / Marion Kelsh:** a stranger who told him the answer to the question he came with, in a corridor, in nine minutes, because he was honest about having no reason to be there. **He is the only person in ten days who got something from a stranger by standing still.**
- **Elias / Perry Loach:** a second no, better than the first, and then a yes when it came from a letterhead. **And the cost: the Director's office asked, and he said yes, and the record says his grade field was left empty at the counter's insistence, and the empty is in the file, and the empty is how he exists.**
- **Elias / Cliff Sowerby:** a knock, a letter, four lines, a counterfoil, and a sentence about a Tuesday. **It is the volume's oldest debt and it is paid and it cost nothing and he is not going to be thanked for it either.**
- **Mara / Ivo:** a sentence and a distance. She is the person who initialed his comparison and who would have to countersign the sentence he has to write on Wednesday, and neither of them has mentioned that, and both of them know it.
- **Mara / Anja:** four lines and a cost said before a signature, and a signature that cannot be undone, and a panel that is still taped on a case because it belongs to a woman of fifty-four who is not Museum staff.
- **Mara / Tully / Roth:** the Registry now has a name in this building and a refusal on a form with a reference on it, and the Registry is still not wrong about anything, and the mat is on a floor in a gallery because of it.

# CHARACTER STATE — Volume 02, Batch 0002 (Chapters 61–70), as of Friday 5 February 2025, about 23:30

**Ages are canon and unchanged. States as of the close of Chapter 70 / Friday 5 February 2025. Read against `state/continuity.md`, which is the authority.**

## Elias Venn, 32

**Where he is.** **The same rented room above a laundrette in Low Ward.** No post, no access, no employer, no card, no key, no radio, no torch. Barred from the Museum as from 1 December 2024, save attendance at a hearing or inquiry at which he is a party or a witness, on notice, in company, not before eight. He has been **inside the building on eleven days in twenty-four** — 13, 18, 19, 20, 21, 22, 25(walk sheet only, not the building), 26, 27, 28 and 3–5 February — and on nine of them he was there because a competent person produced him, and on four of them he paid a pound and signed the visitors' book as a member of the public, which is the only door in this city that has ever opened anything for him without anybody deciding to.

**What he has.** A pound. **A plastic wallet with four things in it: the Museum's own card with FRANK DUNNING's name on it that came back marked undeliverable, a bank statement, a receipt, and a counter receipt from Nether Kiln Lane on the back of which a woman of about twenty-seven wrote four lines in her own hand — and that receipt is the only piece of paper in this city that has the fifth of December 2024 on it.** A nine-page notebook, thirty-five pence, with a rule now at the top of the first page in his own hand: **a fact a person gives me on a street is theirs until it is in a file. Do not write it down.** A cardboard folder on a shelf between a boiler and a chair that is deliberately not in a Museum file. A municipal remittance on the first of the month at cost centre M.Q.S. 4 for a post that no longer exists. An eighteen-year-old letter on a shelf.

**Skill state. Stage 1 — Echo, and he has read nothing in twenty-four days and nobody has asked him to.** The mat in the Hall is about four feet from where he stood on 22 December and he has not been near it. The bell is silent. **The instrument he is best at is the one thing he has stopped using: he is being handed facts by competent people and he is refusing to write them down, and he has done it twice — the sixteen on 25 January, the word on 22 January — and both refusals cost him something and both are on the page.**

**The growth, and it is the only thing in this batch that is one.** He has **stopped collecting.** He did not take the sixteen. He did not take the word. He did not go and look at the public register. He stood on a pavement in Broad Street with nothing in his hands at all for the first time since 6 October. He wrote nothing down for two days after 26 January and for five days before 5 February. **And he has not been embarrassed by it and has not called it a virtue, which is the whole of the difference between a method and a posture.**

**His position, restated in his own words in Chapter 68 and it has not softened:** *the sheet on the table in Sillick Lane is now nothing, and it is going to be nothing on the day Frank Dunning stops keeping it, and he is about sixty-five, and nobody has asked him how he is and nobody is going to.* He also said, and it is on the page: *that is a worse answer than a museum had it, and she is right, and I knew it first.*

**The moral floor is not his and he did not say it.** It was said by a registration officer of thirty-one on a doorstep and a school business manager of fifty-four in a corridor, and he wrote it down afterwards, and he was not in the room for either. **He has had the sentence since December, from Hattie Bramm, and did not give it, and this batch is the first time the case has heard it from somebody who is neither him nor her.**

**What he is frightened of, specifically, and it has changed shape twice.** Not the post, and not the pound. **First: that the practice is portable. A mat can be bought for two pounds, and a practice with two pounds in it has no address, and the number of people who can be hurt went up by a pound shop.** Second: **that the only document with the fifth of December on it is in his own wallet and dies with him, and that the Museum has just minuted that it will not take the plastic wallet off Frank Dunning for exactly the same reason, and that he knew it first and had said nothing.**

**What he is holding that he has never said to anybody.** The bell at 11:52 on 11 November 2024. Not referred to in this batch and not escalated. **And the four seconds of a name, in October, which he cannot identify and never will.**

**His stop word. He has none. Nobody has given him one. On 22 January 2025 a man of twenty-five offered him one in a corridor and he said no, and it is on the page, and the refusal cost him the only thing anybody had offered him in eight weeks, and the reason is: *a thing that can be stopped by the man who is doing it has not been stopped, it has been interrupted*, and he wrote that down in a library corridor in December off a registrar and she took the credit for it.** The practice's word is a permission and it belongs to a street: *anybody taking part may stop at any point.* It has not become his.

## Mara Iles, 28

**What she did in this batch, in order, and every item of it is a document or a refusal that is a document.** She **read a public register into existence and refused it in front of the man who proposed it**, on 26 January, at about half past ten: *go and look at the register, I am not going to stop you and there is nothing I could do* — and then told him why, and the why is the case's argument, and the last line of it is *you want me to be the person who goes to the public register and gets four hundred and twelve names off it, and I have spent nine days not putting a name on a door mat, and I am not going to do it in nine seconds from a terminal in a post office because a man of twenty-five had a good idea at a screen.* She **wrote four lines to S. Tully on 22 January, the first letter this building has ever sent to Northglass that was not a reply to something, and asked a question that is not a classification question**, and it is dated 6 February in effect. She **put a dead man's name on a Museum document on 4 February, in her own hand, in a room, with no form for it and no field for it, and it is the first person's name this institution has put on a document on purpose in about a hundred and twenty years.** She **refused to lend a mat to a street**, in a corridor, at about four o'clock on 22 January, and the reason is the count: *a duty means somebody is responsible, and responsible means it turns up on a schedule, and a schedule means it gets counted on a Friday by somebody whose job it is.* She **found, in her own handwriting, that she had written a card about a mat that describes every mat, and said the sentence before anybody asked her.** She **stopped a systems technician from turning a binder into a prosecution** and used the word *prosecution* to mean *don't*, for the first time in this case. She **refused to file Doreen Sarn's seventh refusal and put it on the board**, with the reason: *a list of people who have refused something is a list of people.* She **put her own name on a four-line minute about having seen a practice she has never been to**, and said why in nine seconds: *it is not much.*

**Her six positions, all standing, and none reversed in this batch:** she will not be his witness · she will not open a box in a room with a camera in it · she will not take a number out of a notebook · she will not be helped to ask the Registry's question · she will not tell him what is on a card in a public room and not a Museum file · **and she will not use the public register, which is the sixth and the largest and is new in this batch.**

## Ivo Pell, 25

**What he did, and it is the smallest set of acts in the volume and the engine of the twenty-four days.** He **wrote four sentences about a person to a stranger** and says he would rather do four hundred envelopes than sit in a systems office with a mug. He **found clause seven, sent the query, got the answer, and minuted it as a null.** He **found item 6 of the terms of reference**, which is the only thing in that document with no subject and has been doing nothing since 18 October, and the letter went out on it. He **read a binder and transcribed fourteen mornings in ink**, because of his own condition of 1 December, which is honoured by not photographing and is better for it. He **read the last sentence on the top of a file he had looked at four thousand times and found that the same feed is not the source of the electoral roll, and said *I have got a systems login and four years and a Saturday and nobody is paying me to know what a field means, and there it was the whole time*, and that it made the case smaller and worse.** He **read the fourth column, `OCCUPANT (PRIOR)`, on 3 February and said the sentence that is the finding of the second movement: the district keeps the old name and we do not.** He **did the arithmetic on three documents and demoted the fifth of December in front of a registrar and a systems index**, and the honest part of the page is that the fifth is the one with a person in it and he knows that. He **proposed the public register, was refused, and did not go.** He **offered a man a word and was refused, and did not push, and did not look at him again.** He **got down on his knees on the floor of a public gallery to count the lays in a knot and said *the one thing I have learned in seven weeks is that a thing stops being a rope the second time somebody writes a word about it in a room with a date in the minute*, and refused to go and open a box of minutes from 1935 on the strength of a Saturday systems day.** He has not been thanked for any of it and is not going to be.

## Anja Ward, 53

**What she did, and it is four decisions and each one had the cost said aloud before it, in that order, minuted in that order.** She **signed a letter to a council department on 18 January** and told the room the cost first: *a records officer four miles away is going to write down that this Museum cannot read its own history, and that sentence is going to be in a file in her building, and she may show it to us, and it is not a hostile act and she will not intend it as one.* She **held a meeting in a public gallery on 20 January at one o'clock and said the sentence about 1902 out loud in front of about ninety people** — the bell cast in 1902, *Municipal Works*, no such body, no file, and *I found out on the thirtieth of December and I am the Director* — and told them the cost of the mat in the same breath, again, having said it first in December. She **wrote to Hattie Bramm on the Wednesday afternoon and put the actual question to her in plain words before asking it, and then handed the telephone to a registrar of twenty-eight** so that the person with standing to hold the thing would hear the answer. She **decided on 27 January that the Museum will not hold the sheet**, and said the cost first: *we are going to give away the only thing we have that they do not have, because holding it would make us dangerous to them, and I would like it recorded that I know exactly how that sounds*, and added the arrogance herself: *we are choosing it for them, which is a piece of arrogance I would like in the minute, and I would like the sub-group to strike it out if it thinks it is worse than what it replaces.* Nobody struck it out. **And she read a dead man's name into the record in a room on 5 February and specified the order she wanted it read in, and the order is that a woman of eighty-one said it first.**

**She is not an ally and has not become one.** The relationship is a line in a visitors' book, a four-line minute, and *we are going to be a party to the matter and not a bystander, and will answer for that in due course.*

## Doreen Sarn, 78

**The seventh refusal, in four words, on a piece of A4, returned by post in two days: *No. Ask somebody else.*** Nobody had to go to Ferry Bank. It is on a board and not in a file, and the reason a registrar of twenty-eight gave in a corridor is that there are seven of them now and a list of people who have refused something is a list of people. She is not coming, she was asked anyway, and the seventh refusal cost a letter and four words and nothing else.

## Hattie Bramm, 54

**She came, and she was not controlled by anybody, and what she did in the building was the best two minutes of the second movement.** She **found the join in the rope in about nine seconds** — a sheet bend, a sailor's knot, on a landlocked floor, done in about four seconds in about 1935 — and then **stopped the conversation from becoming beautiful**: *don't you dare make those two the same number. That rope is a hundred and forty years old. I have got a hundred and forty names in a drawer in number fourteen and I am not giving them to anybody, and if the two hundreds and forties get put in the same sentence in this building by some well-meaning person with a pen, then the rope becomes a piece of evidence and it is not, and my hundred and forty become a piece of furniture and they are not.* She **declined, in a public room, in front of a Director who minuted it, to have the workers' panel taken down**, and gave the reason, and the reason is the chapter: the correction will go under it, nobody will ever read it, and the person who reads it will be somebody in about 2040 who lifts a thing off a case. She **said the thing about the coat pocket out loud in a room of about twenty people without naming him and without looking at him once** — *every single thing that has gone right in this business has gone right because a competent person said a true thing out loud in a room while a man with no standing was in it, and he was in all of them, and he has never once been asked to account for it.* And she **refused the sheet to the Museum on a telephone in four minutes and gave the reason before she was asked**: *a list of people is not evidence until it is in a file, and the second it is in a file it can be used — and the day a Museum has got that sheet, it is a museum that knows who lives on that street, and you cannot un-know it.* **She has still given away nothing. She still owes him nothing. The debt changed shape and she is the only person in the case who has said so out loud.**

## Frank Dunning, about sixty-five

**He rang the front desk, which he has never done, and he read a board in his own museum about his own sheet of A4 and nobody in the building had thought to ask him.** He **put a second mat down the street at two pounds and did not tell anybody, and then said on a telephone that he could not work out what that was, and he has been a gate clerk for thirty-one years and has never not been able to work out what a thing is.** He **said the floor of the practice**: it is three weeks and three days old, it is a piece of A4 in a plastic wallet on a table in his hall, and it dies with him; nobody is going to come and take it off him; and that is the correct arrangement, because *if somebody came and took that sheet off this table then it would be a thing somebody was keeping, and then it would be a department, and then I would be a man in a department, and about nine people on a pavement would be people in a department, and it would be finished.* And: **I have not been asked a question about this since it started. Not by the museum, not by the school, not by the woman at the counter, not by you. That is the best three weeks I have had in about nine years and I am sixty-five.** **He is still not frightened and not reassured and not distressed, and the reason is unchanged: nobody has ever asked him a question about it.**

## Marion Kelsh, 54 — St Anselm's (Hall Lane)

**She refused the practice for a school before she was asked twice, in about four minutes, in the right order, and gave the arithmetic and not a moral position: a school has about three hundred and eighty doors and not one of them is anybody's.** She **said she would have the mat in the gallery back more than anything in that conversation and is not going to ask**, and said *I'd take the mat back and I'd not put it in a corridor* and *the mat in the gallery can stay there for ever*. She **refused the Museum the use of her premises inventory and refused before she was asked twice**, and then **refused the arithmetic**: *you cannot put two hundred and forty next to four hundred and twelve; the difference between those two numbers is a number, and a difference is an adjudication.* And **she stated the batch's moral floor in a corridor and asked for it not to be argued about.** **And she said the thing that is the chapter: *the ones with no name in any system cannot be counted, and cannot be listed, and cannot be reached, and cannot be found later, and every attempt this case has made to turn it into a number has produced a worse number.***

## Mairin Devlin, 31 — registration officer (new in this batch)

**A doorstep person with a satchel and about eleven forms in it, and the only person in the chain who writes a name at a place by hand.** She **filled in a form on a hall table in about nine minutes for a woman of about sixty who has never been on anything and did not know the word register**, and told her it was not a punishment and that she could vote at a polling station with a photograph and a utility bill and it was a longer door into the same room. She **said the moral floor once and would not say it again**: *there is no form in this country for a person at a house that no system has got a name for. I have looked. I am the person who would know.* And she said the sentence the whole case is built on and said it on a doorstep: **the only version of a house that persists is a person who wrote it down and did not throw it away.** **She is not a villain, is not a mystic, is not frightened and is not a friend, and she has never been asked a question by anybody in this case and has not asked for anything.**

## Mrs Toft, about fifty — records officer, district addressing service, Marsh Lane

**Six years in post and still the only person in this case who has never been asked for anything, and in this batch she was asked for everything.** She **telephoned unasked** because the clause does not say how a query reaches an office. She **asked three questions in the right order in eleven seconds and the third one was the one that matters: whether the answer contains a person's name.** She **produced the answer in four minutes** and said *nobody has ever asked me for either of those* in the tone of somebody stating a fact about a drawer. She **released a binder as a subscriber copy on a letterhead letter and refused a single morning on request, now and ever.** She **has kept a nine-entry record of issue on her own initiative for six years and the ninth entry is a museum**, and it is in the file. And **she took a case apart in about four minutes on a telephone and made it smaller**: *this file is not a register of persons. It is not a source for the register of electors, the council tax base, the electoral roll, or any statutory return. It is a distribution list with a name on it.* She still has not been thanked.

## Roy Tilley, about fifty — postman, FLS-2

**Going to be moved on 9 February 2025 and does not know it and will not find out, and the reason is the best moral architecture in the case: *if I find out, then on the ninth of February I'm a man who knew, and then the office knows I've been asking.*** He offered sixteen addresses and dates in his own hand with no name on it, and was refused, and **did not mind and did not push**. He says the sheet is not his, that the rack has about nine hundred in it, that they are kept two years for audit and then binned, and that **his goes into the rack on 9 February 2025 and will be a piece of paper in a skip by 2027 and nobody will ever know it was different from the other eight hundred and ninety-nine.** And: *you're the only person I've ever talked to about this who's told me the truth about what he's got. You know how many haven't?*

## Mara Iles, and the comparison sheet

**The second entry in a schedule of collections that has been incomplete since 9 December, and it is not an object.** A dead man's name, a street, a date, a column head, a registrar's initials and a witness's, transcribed by hand, no photograph, no exposure, condition of 1 December not engaged.

## Relationships, end of Volume 02 Batch 0002

- **Elias / Mara:** a working arrangement in which **she has now refused him the one thing he could have got in an afternoon, lawfully, for nothing — the public register — and told him to go and do it himself, and he did not go.** He has withheld nothing from her except the bell. There is no romance and there is no confession and there are no rooms. **The last time he was in a room with her they were arguing about whether a dead man's name belongs on a Museum document and she won and he wrote it down.**
- **Elias / Ivo:** a friendship in which Ivo has now **offered him a word and been refused, and offered him a reason and been used, and both of them know the other one is right.** Neither of them has said thank you and it is not going to be said and it does not need to be.
- **Elias / Hattie:** **the debt has changed shape and she has said so and he has not been able to answer it.** She spoke for him in a room without naming him and then declined to have a panel taken down and then refused a Director on a telephone. **He is the reason and he is furniture and she has said that out loud and he has not once asked her for anything since 18 December.**
- **Elias / Frank Dunning:** a knock at 88 on 13 December that turned into a working arrangement in which a man of sixty-five telephones a Museum for the first time in his life and a man of thirty-two reads a board in his own museum and says *right*. **Neither of them is frightened and the whole of the relationship is that neither of them has been asked a question by the other and both of them are still talking.**
- **Elias / Marion Kelsh:** a stranger who has now refused him twice before he asked and once after, and who stated the case's moral floor in a corridor, and who is not a moral position and said so.
- **Elias / Anja:** authorisation without information, then a decision that took the only instrument away from him, and *she is right and I knew it first*, and that is the whole of it.
- **Elias / Mrs Toft:** he rang her once, in the first week of January, and got more out of four minutes than out of a month, and she has never been thanked and will say so if anybody tries.
- **Elias / Doreen Sarn:** her seventh refusal, four words, by post, and he did not go to Ferry Bank and did not ask.
- **Elias / the bell at 11:52:** untouched, unspoken, and not referred to.
