# CHARACTER STATE — Volume 02, Batch 0004 (Chapters 81–90), as of Sunday 25 April 2025, about 23:30

## Elias Venn, 32

**Barred from the Museum since 1 December 2024 as a security matter, save attendance at a hearing or inquiry at which he is a party or a witness, on notice, in company, not before eight. No post, no access, no employer, no card, no key, a pound, the tenth of a series of pocket notebooks, a plastic wallet with four things in it, and a municipal remittance on the first of the month at cost centre M.Q.S. 4 for a post that does not exist. He is on a payroll and the payroll is the reason he is a legal neighbour, and that is now on a statutory intake in Northglass in a registrar's mouth with a date on it, and he has told nobody except a page.**

**What changed in this batch, and none of it is a power:**
- **He was asked a question by a statutory officer about himself and answered it truthfully and was wrong.** *Have you ever had any connection with this office.* No. And there is one line against his name in box eleven of the intake, in his own hand, dated 6 February 2025, the only handwritten entry in that box in nineteen years. **He remembered signing it. He was not caught out. He has written on the page that the difference between a man who lies to a statutory officer and a man who does not know that filling in a box is the same as putting on a tie is the whole of what he has learned in twenty weeks, and that he has been careful with other people's fields for twelve weeks and six days and has never once thought about his own.**
- **He read nine pages out loud, in order, for an hour and ten minutes, in a label room, with the author present, no copy, no exposure, on a written authority** — and **he lost nothing, and he checked, and the check was done by somebody else, which is the new part.** He has said on the page that the reason he had not been read by a second person for eleven weeks was a rule, and the rule has now produced an error nobody caught for eleven weeks.
- **He carried a dead man's name in his head for eleven weeks and said nothing, and a woman of eighty-one said it first at a door in January and was told *Who?*** He has written that this is a fact about him and not about her, and **he did not write the name down, and the omission is on the page, and the rule held when it was hardest.**
- **He agreed, in a corridor at about twenty to eleven on 15 April, with two other people, that nobody was going to ask Vera Pilling anything about the case — because one of them was going to want to.** He did not ask. That is the fifth time in nineteen weeks and the first time he has agreed to it in advance with anybody.
- **He is no longer the only person in the case who has nothing and no longer the only one who has said no.** Six people are owed nothing; four people have refused something in three weeks. He wrote all six down on 25 April and said he has not spoken to one of them since 18 February.
- **He has been in five rooms in nine weeks that he was not asked to be in, and he has stopped counting, and he has written down that it stopped being a method about six weeks ago and became a habit, and that a habit is going to cost him something eventually and he does not know what.**
- **He let somebody else read the ninth notebook and did not offer it.** The ninth notebook ran out on 11 April and cost thirty-five pence and the tenth cost forty pence. The nine pages are on a shelf between a boiler and a chair and they are the only notebook in the world that anybody in this case might want.
- **He said no to a fifth thing** — a word, offered on 20 February in a hall, in front of about nine people, and it was a rule that belongs to a street. Not restaged in this batch.

**He still has no stop word and nobody who had the standing to offer one did. His question has changed shape twice in this batch and neither change is a resolution: *who keeps it* became, on 16 April, *nobody, is now on a form in Morningside*, and on 23 April it became *somebody who checks it*, and he has written down that he does not yet know whether that is better and is not going to write it down as better.**

## Mara Iles, 28

**Senior Conservator and Accession Registrar, five years in this building. `I do not know what you are for` stands.** In this batch she:
- **said the cost out loud before every decision, in a corridor, in a Board room, on a hearing, in a room in Morningside, and on a pavement in April**, and in every case said it before the opinion.
- **wrote a nil return in four lines and two names and said the cost of it in the document: a party which holds nothing and says so is a party that can be asked to produce nothing for ever after** — and she stopped the Director from putting a policy sentence in the fourth line, because *if your sentence is in line four then the return is a policy; if it is not, it is a return.*
- **stopped the schedule of collections going to the conciliation service under clause 6, and her reason is not caution: a party that gives an office a third thing it did not ask for stops returning nil and starts supplying.**
- **said the cost of a schedule entry out loud, and it is the batch's smallest and hardest sentence: there is no panel for a schedule entry, and this building has exactly one cover it has ever been able to offer anybody and there is not going to be a second one.**
- **said the payroll out loud in a room in Northglass, having asked S. Tully in advance whether it was her sentence**, and the thing she had been carrying alone since 6 February is now on a statutory intake.
- **decided not to read the ninth notebook, and gave the reason herself, in about nine seconds, and it is the best decision anybody in this case has made since November: everything she reads becomes a document in a museum file the moment it is read, and the minute of 27 January says this Museum holds no record of what happens on that street, and a second reading by her would have been the first one that broke it.** She has read one of his notebooks and she is not going to read two.
- **refused to answer the object-or-place question in a room with a letting agent in it, and gave a new reason, and the reason is procedural and not caution: *I am the person who will have to write it down*** — and then said the volume's answer to the other two parts of the question: **a practice is a permission; a permission cannot be located; they are on a step and not in a building; there is no instrument and there is not going to be one; and not one instrument in this country can hold a promise to say nothing.**
- **asked Vera Pilling to write a name down and was refused twice**, said the cost out loud on a pavement, and then said the thing: the card will carry the name and under it that it is unsupported beyond a district's prior value and that a person who knew the man declined to be recorded — **and the reason is not on it, because the reason is hers and because a reason on a card is a handle** — and it will be wrong on purpose, **and it is the best I can do with a no.**
- **decided that nothing on the table in Low Ward is described in a room where a determination is being made, and said the word *hook* and said she was not going to un-say it in Northglass**, and S. Tully said that was the correct answer and would have given it to her if she had not worked it out.

## Anja Ward, 53

**Director, nineteen years. She says the cost out loud before she decides and it is minuted in that order.** In this batch she:
- **said the volume's own position in a van on the way to Northglass, before they went in: this Museum is the last institution in the chain, and a door mat is not an institution and is not in the chain at all, and a door mat is the only thing anybody in this business has ever been able to hand to a person, and that is the whole of the trouble with it** — and then, twenty months later than she could have said it: **now I find it is the reason I have spent nine weeks failing, and I would like it in a minute somewhere, and I do not know which minute, and that is probably the right state for it to be in.**
- **asked for her own sentence to be put back into a document and said on the record that she did not need four seconds to agree to it.**
- **was refused in a corridor by a woman of fifty-four and did not argue, and said she had been let off rather than refused and that she could tell the difference.**
- **proposed, and did not get adopted, that the Museum's minute of 27 January be directed somewhere** — because she does not know who it is for, and in nineteen years she has never not known who a document was for.
- **put the cost of the last decision of the batch out loud: the form has to exist, a person has to hold it, a date has to be at the end of it, and not one of the four of us can be the first person.** And the finding, in a lobby, with the door open: **the first person is not in this van.**

## Ivo Pell, 25

**Systems technician, four years in post, the Museum's named subscriber contact. His condition of 1 December 2025 — countersignature at the point of making — is in force and it bit in Chapter 89 and it worked exactly as written.** In this batch he:
- **asked where has to be, properly, in three parts, having written it out and refused to read from it**, and got the answer he had said in a hall in February he would not be the one to pick: **there is none and there is not supposed to be one.**
- **got the second part of the fight, which is not his and which he recognised: a postman's knowledge of a district is the only instrument anybody in this case has produced that scales, and it is not a record and it is not admissible, and the man who produced it said both of those before anybody asked.**
- **asked a Director for a minuted line, put a card over a camera lens with a date on it, and did not countersignature anything because nothing was made** — and Elias Venn wrote on the page that he has been right about a rope in public for five weeks and has had a room every time he has been right and has been in none of them, and that this time he was in the room, and that nobody mentioned it and nobody mentioned the rope.

## Sonia Boulton, about thirty-nine, MRICS — letting agent, Morton Hay & Baker, 11 Pemberton Row

**Eleven years in post. Hardback book, every visit since about 2019, in her hand, with a date. Refused seven things since the autumn and the seventh is the Museum.** In this batch she:
- **served about forty notices on the morning of 1 April, by hand, at the property, on the earliest date permitted, saying *notice* before she said anything else, and wrote in the book at every door, and refused the book again — kindly — to a woman with a satchel**, and said the thing about refusing that is new: **if I am introduced then in about four years there is a document with two professionals' names on it about one street, and a name in a field is the only thing this business has ever caught anything with, and I have spent eleven years watching a person get got.**
- **answered two parts of the location question and refused the third in about nineteen minutes, and gave the batch's best professional formulation: a practice with a shop in it is portable; a practice with a landlord in it is not; and that is not a moral, it is a fact about what a practice needs — and there is no version of that answer where it comes from her, and the only person in the room who can be wrong about half of it is the registrar, and she knows it.**
- **came to a room in Morningside about five weeks after saying she would not, and gave the same reason as in February, and has declined to give a better one.**

## S. Tully, about forty-five — assessor, Quiet Accord Registry

**Nineteen years in post, `QA/CR/1426`. In one Saturday she said the reason a question about a room cannot be listed, said what a listing would require, noticed in February and mentioned in March that there is no household in this case, asked a man whether he had ever had a connection with her office and found one line in his own hand in a box that had never been used, said the thing that keeps the whole of it out of a registry record, and issued the shortest determination of her career with `countersigned: none` on its face because there was nobody in the building to countersign it.** She is right about every part of it and she is not a friend and she is not frightened and she did not have to do any of it.

## H. Marchant, about fifty-six — conciliation officer, Morningside — NEW IN THIS BATCH, NAMED

**Nineteen years. Named for the first time in Chapter 83 and she is the source of the volume's structural finding.** She read the Museum's own minute out loud at the hearing it created; **asked the parties the question and then asked herself the question out loud in the room in the order she always does it, and said the practice is not the matter;** wrote a **note** rather than issuing a determination on purpose, because a determination is public and a note is not; said the word **forum** and that no body in this city has a jurisdiction, a form or an officer whose duty it is to hear a person say that a landlord has taken their ability to be recognised as the neighbour of an address; **refused to give a date because a date is the only thing anybody has wanted from her since 6 December and it is not hers to give**; asked **is there a household in this matter**; said nine people did one thing that was wrong and every step of it was correct; and said **there is no form for asking a man whether he wants to be in a room and I have checked and I am the person who would know.** She is not a villain and she is not an ally and she has done the most useful thing anybody has done in the business in twenty weeks.

## Frank Dunning, about sixty-five — 88 Sillick Lane

**Keeper of the practice, keeper of the table, keeper of the kettle and of the running of it at three o'clock. Owed nothing, on no list, tenancy to the twenty-second of August. In this batch he:**
- **said why Vera Pilling is not in his hall in about nine seconds: she is the only one of us who has paid for this with her body and the rest of us have paid for it with a Tuesday.**
- **decided the handout of a registrar's four lines in about four seconds and refused to hand them to anybody who had not put a hand out**, because a handout to everybody is a leaflet and a leaflet makes the building the author, and his Director's minute says this Museum is not going to be the author of anything about that street. He did not count who took one and **said he has counted things at a gate for about forty years and is not going to start at sixty-five.**
- **asked, unasked, at about ten to four on 22 March, whether about nine people wanted to be in it — the answer was allowed to be no and he was not going to ask why** — and found out in about four minutes that not one of them had ever been asked and that not one of them knew there was anything to say yes to, and said **somebody is going to have to ask us properly, not you, not a museum, not a clipboard, somebody with a job, and they had better be able to hear a no.**
- **asked a registration officer to use one four-word question and told her why: because I am the man with the table, and if somebody asks about me they are asking a man of sixty-five whether his feelings are all right, and they are, and it takes forty minutes.**
- **said the fourth rule before anybody asked him, and said he had been waiting since 28 February for somebody to make him: the fourth rule is *somebody is keeping it*.** He is not going to be the one, and he would have said yes on 4 January if anybody had asked, and he is not going to be asked now. **He has now said that the arrangement is correct and the worst in the world in that order twice, and has not been wrong once in two months.**

## The man of fifty-two, of a first-floor flat on the east side of Sillick Lane — NEW IN THIS BATCH, UNNAMED AND TO STAY UNNAMED

**Twenty-six years in the flat. Never on a sheet. Never asked a question in three months. He drafted the boundary in about four minutes with a bolt pen because nobody asked him, and changed his mind in the last twenty seconds of the four minutes because nobody asked him, and refused a register in about nine seconds for a reason nobody else in this case has given: a list of people is a list of people and a list of houses is worse, and there are about nine empty flats on that street and nothing anywhere in this city says what it was.** He is not a villain and he is not a fool and he is the only person in the batch who arrives at the moral floor of the whole case from a different road and gets there first.

## The woman of forty-four, first-floor flat on the east side of Sillick Lane — NEW IN THIS BATCH, UNNAMED

**Nineteen years of rent, every month, no gap, into a counter in a book in an office in Pemberton Row that is destroyed on a Friday. She wants the money, she said she is owed it by nobody before anybody said it, and she is right, and there is no fund for what she is owed, and she did not sit down.** She is the hardest person in this batch for the case to be honest about and nobody in the case managed to be cruel to her.

## Roy Tilley, about fifty — postman

**Not on that round and does not know it. Nobody in the case is entitled to tell him and no instrument in the city can tell him without his asking.** He said the only thing anybody has said in this case that is about the future — a sequence and not a list, four hundred doors in order, one time, on a day a man is standing there anyway — **and gave the flaw himself before he was asked, in about eleven seconds: a postman is a council employee, what he knows about a household is not evidence, and if he says it in a room he is a council officer reporting on a household, which is the thing that has already happened to about nine people in this ward, and it happened with a form, and the form was correct.** He will not write it down. He is the one instrument in this case with a hole in it and the hole is Elias Venn.

## Mrs Toft, about fifty — records officer, district addressing service, Marsh Lane

**Six years in post, nine entries in a record of issue, eight of them her own retention audits, asked for one thing in six years until Friday 18 April.** She came in her own time and refused a bus fare twice. She checked nine pages in seventy minutes and found the only thing wrong in them, and it was a day of the week, and she said **a date is a fact about the world and a weekday is a fact about whoever typed it** and that nobody has ever asked anybody to check a weekday because we all think we can do them in our heads, and that she has been wrong about two in six years and both times it mattered. And then: **if I can be wrong about one, anybody can be wrong about one, and the only way anybody finds out is if a second person reads it — and there is no form for *is there a second person*.** She is the batch's answer to *who keeps it* and it is *somebody who checks it*. She has still not been thanked.

## Hattie Bramm, 54

**In this batch, in a corridor, on Friday the ninth of April, and not on a street and not in a room and not at a hearing.** She has a hundred and forty names in a drawer in number fourteen. **She has refused this Museum twice — 23 December 2024 over a printed form, and 27 January 2025 in about eleven seconds — and this Museum has refused her once, in writing, in a corridor, in December, and it was correct, and she is not refused a third time by a museum and is not given any number that is not in `state/continuity.md`.** She came out of a door when a Director came up the hill and said **you have not asked me a third time, you have come and stood in a corridor and said you were not entitled to, which is a different thing, and I have been doing this for thirty-one years and I can tell the difference between somebody being refused and somebody being let off, and nobody has ever let me off.** She said the moral floor in eleven words, and said it was a registration officer's and not hers, and that the Director was not going to write it down **and that is why I said it**; and she claimed the whole of it from a corridor in 1994 — **the hundred and forty are in a drawer and there is no list of the nine and there never will be, and you cannot take a list of a thing nobody kept** — and nobody can check the claim and it does not matter, because it is true whichever of them said it first, and the two women who said it have never met. And she refused a register, and would not give the hundred and forty to a museum or a satchel or a postman. **The seven refusals on the board on the ground floor of a building on a hill are Doreen Sarn's, and not all of them are hers, and that is said on the page in April.**

## Doreen Sarn, 78

**Not present, not asked to be present, not coming. Asked for the eighth time since November, by letter, on 24 March, and the letter said no reply was required, and she did not reply, because there was nothing to reply to.** A thing with nothing in it is not a refusal and is not going to be recorded as one: **eight requests, seven refusals, and the difference is one woman who did not answer a letter that did not require an answer, and the board still says seven, and it is correct, and in about four years somebody will read that board and think she said no seven times and said nothing the eighth.** Her seven refusals are on a board and not in a file, and the seventh came back by post in four words on Friday 29 January 2025.

## The woman of about thirty-four at number four in a road called Broad Street

**NOT NAMED, NOT DESCRIBED SPECIFICALLY ENOUGH TO FIND, AND NOT TO BE NAMED.** She appears once in this batch, in Chapter 87, as the person who told him, in a library, on 7 April 2025, that Vera Pilling was going back on the fifteenth. **He did not ask her how she knew and she did not say, and that is the whole of what happened between those two people and neither of them will ever refer to it again.** She is on the roll. She is owed nothing. **They are still the only two people in this city in exactly the same position and they still have not met, and he has written that down on 25 April and that he has not spoken to her since 18 February.**

## Mara Iles and the hundred and fourteen pages

**Untouched. She read them in twenty-two minutes on 11 December 2024 and asked no question and took no copy and wrote a minute with a date on it that he made her give him so he could not move it. She has been asked once in this batch, for a second reading, and said no in about two seconds, and it is the fifth time she has refused him something and every one of the five was right.**

## Relationships, end of Volume 02 Batch 0004

- **Elias / Mara:** a working arrangement in which **she decided not to read his notebook, and gave the reason herself, in a room, in about nine seconds, and he did not argue and has written down that every one of her five refusals to him has been right.** No romance. No confession. **The best decision anybody in this case has made since November was made by her, about herself, and it protected him and her and the minute of 27 January at once.**
- **Elias / Frank Dunning:** a working arrangement in which **the man with the table has now asked him a question and told him the fourth rule before being asked, and has told him twice that the arrangement is correct and the worst in the world in that order, and has been asked by a registration officer to use one four-word question so that nobody would have to spend forty minutes on his feelings.** They are not friends and nobody is owed anything and that is correct.
- **Elias / Mrs Toft:** **a stranger who was asked, in writing, with a purpose, to check his handwriting, and who found the one thing wrong in it, and who refused a bus fare twice.** He has not thanked her. **She is the only person he has met in this case who has told him that the thing he was proudest of — not keeping a notebook — was a risk, and the person who told him the first time was a solicitor and the person who has now shown him the mechanism is a records officer with a wall calendar.**
- **Elias / the man of fifty-two:** **they have not spoken and he is not going to speak to him.** A man drafted a boundary nobody asked him to and would not sign it because nobody asked him, and that is the whole of the case, and Elias Venn is the man who has spent twenty weeks asking everybody questions and has not once been the person who was not asked.
- **Elias / the Bell at 11:52:** untouched, unspoken, and not referred to in this batch. **The bell has now been silent for one hundred and thirty-three days and no bell is on a step and nobody has ever asked the two of them about each other and nobody is going to.**

---

# Character State — after Volume 02 Batch 0005 (Chapters 91–100) and at the END OF VOLUME 02

**Ages are canon and unchanged: Elias Venn 32 · Mara Iles 28 · Ivo Pell 25 · Anja Ward 53 · Hattie Bramm 54 · Doreen Sarn 78 · Perry Loach *a night cleaner in his early sixties* · Cliff Sowerby 74 · Margaret Priest about fifty · Mrs Farrier 58 · Fenn surname only · Field Officer Marsh · Vera Pilling 81 · Marion Kelsh 54 · S. Tully about forty-five · Mrs Toft about fifty · Roy Tilley about fifty · R.M. · Denise Hargreaves · Sonia Boulton about thirty-nine · Frank Dunning about sixty-five.**

## Elias Venn, 32 — END OF VOLUME 02

**He has refused six things in twenty-four weeks and every one of them was right, and the sixth was offered kindly.** On 27 May a housing officer with a duty, in a corridor, on her own initiative, with no form and nobody writing him down, asked him to sit in on the first of June at Cauldwell Buildings, and he said no, and gave the reason in about eleven seconds without softening it: **the day I am in that building on the first of June, then in about four years there is a man in a room who can say the practice at Sillick Lane was in Cauldwell Buildings on the first of June with a man of thirty-two in it, and he will be right, and I will have put him there. And because seven households are being asked to sign a contract with a landlord and one of the things that has kept this case honest for twenty-four weeks is that nobody in it has ever had a landlord. Put me in a building with a landlord and I am a man with a landlord, and in four years that is a handle and not a witness.** Mara Iles said it was the sixth time and every one was right, and wrote nothing down, because the Registrar of a museum is not anybody's housing officer and there is no box for it.

**He was told to do less, for the first time in the case, and he obeyed.** On Sunday 16 May, in a room above a shop, after one knock on a door at about half past four that two people out of five heard, a man of fifty-two said *do not write that down*, and gave the reason rather than the prohibition, and he obeyed, and wrote down instead **the only page in that notebook with nothing on it except the fact that something was not written: no description of a sound, no adjective, no time to the second, no note about what it might have been, the page not torn out.** He wrote in it that he is obeying a man of fifty-two for the first time in this case and **he does not think the man knows that is the first time.** He records that he noticed something about himself that afternoon and is not going to call it anything yet, **and he wants that written down in the same breath as the thing he has noticed, and not in a separate line, and he has noticed that he keep wanting to write it in a separate line.** The reason he was given is now written down in his own hand, as the sentence he is not going to look for an alternative to: **a thing you cannot check is worth nothing and a thing you can check is worth everything, and that is the entire finding of this case.**

**He broke his own rule on purpose, in a room, and said so afterwards.** On 20 May, in about four minutes, at a table, with about nine people in the room, he read the addresses and the years out of nine Museum Christmas cards and wrote about thirty numbers into the tenth notebook, **having read the third line of the first page of that notebook in front of them while they were talking, and the third line is *do not write it down*.** He wrote no names, because there were none, and he noticed himself not writing them and did not enjoy the noticing. **His own account, in the notebook: a fact a person gives me in a street is theirs until it is in a file, and nine people gave me nine facts in a room and I wrote them down and it is not a file and it is not a record and nobody will ever be able to check it, and I did it because there was a record in the room and it was going home in somebody's coat.** The second person who read it back was a man of seventy-four with a standpipe key, ten days later, and one of the numbers was wrong by at least eight years, **and the error is his and not Cliff Sowerby's and not the woman's.**

**He lost nothing. He wrote that down at the top of the 30 May entry because he had been waiting all day for it, and this is the third time he has had to write it: I did not lose a page, a name, an evening, or the order of any of it, and nothing about the ninth of December is different from what it was on Wednesday, and I have checked, and I have checked by asking somebody else, and the somebody else this time was not a professional with a wall calendar. It was a man of seventy-four with a standpipe key.** The cost curve is unspent at the end of Volume 02. **The first major memory-displacement cost is reserved for Volume 03 and is the only one this volume was allowed to withhold.**

**And he named the thing, once, late, in the notebook, and not in a room, and did not call it anything else and is not going to write it down twice:** *An echo is one room. It is a thing at one object, from one point of view, and it is bounded and it is safe and it cannot be wrong by anybody but me. What I have been doing for a fortnight is not that. This is a thread. And a thread is not a line. A thread is a thing you can lose the end of and somebody else can pick up, and if I hold it by myself I am the only person in the room again, and I have been the only person in the room five times in twenty-five weeks and every one of the five was this case's fault and not mine. So I am not going to hold it by myself.*

**He gave a document away.** He carried a letter to Cliff Sowerby for seven months, from Friday 30 October 2024, unopened, and handed it over at about half past three on Sunday 30 May without any preamble, and nobody made a thing of it and nobody wrote it down. **It is the first time in the case that he has released a document rather than held it, and it is the whole of Thread in a gesture: somebody else can pick up the end of it.** He has not written it down in the notebook either and is not going to.

**He is not glad. He has been not-glad about three things in twenty-five weeks and this is the first one that was his own hand.** His position is unchanged and is not temporary: no post, no access, no employer, no card, no key, a municipal remittance on the first of the month at cost centre **M.Q.S. 4** for a post that no longer exists, which is a payee field and is correct and is the only thing that has kept him a legal neighbour of Low Ward since 8 December 2024, a mother in a laundrette, an eighteen-year-old letter on a shelf, and a woman of about thirty-four in Broad Street who is the only other person in this city in exactly the position he was in on 6 December 2024 and **whom he has still not met.**

## Mara Iles, 28 — Senior Conservator and Accession Registrar

**She asked nine people to sign a sheet whose last line says nobody signs it, and she refused to take the line off.** She came to a hall in Low Ward on her own on 13 May, not as the Museum, with no document in her bag, and said the cost out loud before she asked the question, because in April a man of fifty-two wrote a document nobody had asked him for and changed his mind at the end of it, and she would like the room to know she knows that happened. **The cost: a name against a date on a piece of paper is the strongest thing in this country and the only instrument in this case that has ever caught anything — four or five separate things in twenty-two weeks, every one of them worked — and the same property is a hook.** She asked nine times, said the answer was allowed to be no and would not be written down or counted, gave away the third rule in about nine seconds and would not be thanked for it because it is not hers, **and then somebody did the sum and she refused to account for the five who are unaccounted for, because either she would be writing them down or asking people in front of each other, and that is the same instrument with the manners taken off.** And she said the uncomfortable half before anybody went home: **a piece of A4 in a plastic wallet is not a record, it is the possibility of a record, three names on it will not stop the nine blanks, and anybody who tells this street that it has made a register has not been in her office. A field goes into a system — custodian, retention, disclosure rule, about four hundred people who can read it. A piece of A4 goes in a drawer.**

**She put a false name into a public document on purpose, in front of a stranger, and would do it again.** On 28 May she asked a Director for a note in the schedule of collections at the entry for plinth thirty-four, said in advance that a note in that schedule is a thing she cannot take back because every word in it is a word she is putting into a stranger's hands on purpose, **and that this is the only door in that building that does not lock.** She lost about a third of it and got the rest. She then **refused to give away a sentence that a woman of fifty-four said in a corridor** — started to give it to a label-room finisher, stopped herself, and said it had been said in a corridor in 1994 and on a doorstep in February and in a corridor in April and twice in her own office, **and that she is not going to claim it and not going to give it away.**

**She refused nine names twice, and said the cost first, and recorded that the offer was made.** On 20 May a records officer offered the district's `OCCUPANT (PRIOR)` column; she refused it for the Museum and again for the council, and then said the worst sentence she has said in this case: **it is in my own building on Marsh Lane in a binder that stops on 19 January, and after that there is nothing, and nobody has ever looked at it, and that is the correct place for it.** She asked for the housing officer to put the two refusals on paper and for the true sentence of 20 May to be written in the record. **On 20 May she said the moral floor was said in that room by the records officer, attributed it out loud to a registration officer and not to the person who said it, and did not claim it.**

**And she said the two dates out loud on 27 May, in a corridor, and neither of them is the date anything ends: 1 October and 30 November, sixty days apart, and on the first of December a landlord is free to let four flats to whoever she likes and the question comes back into that room and it will be a worse question because there will be nine people in it who have just done it once.** She has said the cost out loud before every decision she has taken since 12 November and has not made an exception.

## Ivo Pell, 25 — systems technician

**He stopped looking, and said so in a room, and called it the most frightening thing he has ever said.** On 29 May he put nine acquisition dates on a table, said in about four seconds that they all fall on days the FLS-2 delivery round went down this ward, **said the sum is about forty million to one and the sum is remarkable, and then would not say the next sentence, because he has not checked what the next sentence is.** ⚠ **He is the only person in the manuscript who has now admitted in public that he cannot finish a sentence about his own finding, and he is the one whose condition — every photographic exposure of collections material countersigned at the point of making — is still in force and has not been argued about.**

**He also said, for about ten minutes with nobody disagreeing, that the mechanism is lawful and correct at every step:** a cycle date is the first day of each quarter, a block enters programme on a cycle date, notices are served on that day by hand at the property by a person whose name is on the notice, and not one has been challenged and not one was late; **and the machine is a man with a handheld in the last four inches of it, the four inches are about a fortnight, and there is no form for what a man sees when he puts a code on a field at a door and the person at the door is out.** He has been in this case twenty-four weeks, has never been able to find the step that is wrong, and has stopped looking.

## Anja Ward, 53 — Director

**She put a false name into a public document, and refused to put a tidier one there.** On 28 May she read the note twice, said no to about a third of it and gave the rest, and said the words herself: **the word *untraceable* goes, because we did not look, and a note in a public document that says a name is untraceable is a statement about a search that was not made — we looked for a body, we did not look for a person who typed it.** *Removed at the request of a member of the public* also goes, **because it is not true; it was taped on by her, and it stays because she asked, and those are different and one is a favour and the other is a refusal and I will not have a favour in a public document wearing a refusal's clothes.** What stays is the ugly part: **this Museum has been aware of the fault since 30 December 2024 and did nothing about it for seven weeks, and if I leave it out then this note is a public document that is tidier than the truth, and I have sat in a boardroom for nineteen years watching a building get into trouble by being tidier than the truth.** And a fourth thing is deliberately left out: **the requester of the panel must not become part of this Museum's record of itself, and she has refused this building three times and the fourth thing that happened in this case was that this building did not ask her a fourth time.** **Condition two is in force, failed twice on 23 and 29 December 2024, is not amended and is not going to be, and nothing in the last ten chapters touched it.**

## Hattie Bramm, 54 — school business manager

**She printed the panel, taped it on that case on 27 November 2024, and nobody has lifted it.** It is **a hundred and eighty-five days old at the end of this volume**, the tape is the colour of weak tea, and it is the most carefully kept document in the Museum. **She was not asked about it today on 28 May, and has not been asked since 27 April, and it is the fourth time this case could have asked her and did not, and that is written down in a notebook in those words.** She said the sentence about holes in a card in a corridor at about twenty past two on 28 May, in eleven words, and **the registrar started to give it to a label-room finisher and stopped herself and would not give it away.** She is still a woman of about fifty-four in the prose and is not named. **The hundred and forty are in a drawer and she has not been asked an eighth time by a Museum.** ⚠ Her moral-floor claim from a corridor in 1994 still cannot be checked and still does not matter.

## Cliff Sowerby, 74 — NEW IN THIS BATCH, of 14 Sillick Lane

**He is the first person in twenty-five weeks that this case asked a question of, and the reason nobody chose him is that everybody had him filed as a name, and a name is not a person who can be asked.** At number fourteen since about 1974, a fitter's mate at the mill, **he has mended the row's standpipe every autumn for fifty years for a pint he would not take.** In a Museum personnel file of his own from 1974; a line in a hardback book four miles away; a name on a posting sheet for eleven years in a building four miles away with a building's address on it, and neither of them is him; **and the name on a letter carried for seven months in a coat pocket in a rented room in Low Ward and handed to him on 30 May without any preamble.** Nobody in the case had asked him anything since Christmas. He was not asked in April when a woman of yours asked a room full of people what they wanted, **because he has never been on a mat and never wanted to be.**

**He checks things by hand and will not give a year out of his own head.** He has the run of three back yards since about 1975 because the stopcock is under the stairs and has never had a lock on it, and you do not go round knocking when you are going into three back yards for the water. He has knocked at about nine doors on that row over fifty years because they were empty, **and he has never once knocked at number thirty, and the reason is that there has been somebody in it every autumn for as long as I have been on that road.** What he gives the room is three things and not a year: **it is not 1982; he has knocked on that row every autumn since 1974 and there has been somebody in that flat every autumn since the year before he started; and nobody in thirty has ever been in a position to tell him when it started, including the people who have lived in it.** He read about thirty numbers against a piece of A4 in a room, in front of six people, and found the one that was wrong, **and he was the second person to read a thing back, and he did it for no reason anybody can give him.**

**He was given a letter seven months late and did not open it in a room.** **I have lived in this house for fifty-one years and I am not opening a letter from a museum in a room with six people in it, and I will open it on a Monday and I will tell nobody what is in it and you can ring me in a fortnight and I will say that I have opened it.** He put it in his coat pocket. He did not say thank you, and it was not unkind. He is on a public schedule of interim payments and is owed nothing by anybody in this case.

## Vera Pilling, 81 — of 6 Sillick Lane, KEEPER OF THE REGISTER

**She is the answer to the volume's question and she said the reason out loud and asked for it to be written down.** She has been on that street since 1961, and she has seen a great deal come back into this ward and none of it stayed. On 31 May 2025 she brought a hardback notebook of about a hundred and fifty ruled pages, a pound and ten, in a cloth bag, to a room let by the hour. Inside the front cover, in a hand taught in a school in about 1938 and worse since, in ballpoint: **KEPT BY V. PILLING, 6 SILLICK LANE. FROM 31 MAY 2025 UNTIL THE FIRST OF OCTOBER 2025.** A name against a date, and the man of fifty-two said those four words and sat down and said nothing else about it.

**Her qualification, which she said was not a skill and not a job and not a virtue: I am the only person in this room who knows exactly what a piece of paper does to a person after it has been made.** In April she refused twice to write a dead man's name on a card about a house, because in about four years somebody will come to that door with a clipboard and a woman in that flat will have to say no about a man she has never heard of. **She has thought about that every day for six weeks and has worked out what she actually meant, and what she meant is the sentence above. She was the only one of them who had been on the other end of a record, and she did not like it, and she has kept a copy of a pay sheet in a hand of her own in a tin since November 1974 because of it.**

**And why not somebody else: because everybody else in this room wants it to be a good thing.** The man of fifty-two has been waiting since 28 February for somebody to make him say a fourth rule and has still not been asked. The woman with the rota wants the rota to be something. The man with the notebook wants a document. **I do not want a good thing. I want somebody who has been on the wrong end of a piece of paper to be the one holding it, and there is one of those and she is eighty-one and she is not going to be here in four years, and I have said that out loud and I would like it written down.**

**Four terms, and the fourth is the refusal.** It is hers, at number six, in a drawer that does not lock, on purpose. Nobody reads it without her, and she will say the same word to yes and to no. **The day she stops, it stops, and it does not go to the Museum or a council or a solicitor or a man of fifty-two, and she is not appointing anybody, because appointing anybody is how the fourth of January turned into the twenty-eighth of February.** And: **there are no names in it. Not the nine. Not the hundred and forty. Not a person's name at a door, and not the year that was wrong, and not whatever is in a file four miles away with a woman's name on it. It is dates, and times, and door numbers, and how many stopped.** She was told that is useless by a records officer on a bus and by a man of thirty-two and by a woman of fifty-four and by herself, **and the last of those is the one that counts, and she did not need a professional to tell her and she is not going to pretend it took one.**

**The fourth line of the first page is the whole of the volume: *If you are reading this and you are not me, then you are the second person, and that is the only reason it exists.*** And on its own third page: **this is not complete and it cannot be made complete** — it begins on 31 May 2025, there is nothing in it about December 2024 and never will be, because you cannot take a list of a thing nobody kept, **and a gap is a person who stopped and it is the only honest shape a stopped person has.** She was asked whether it was a religion and said no, and when she was asked what it was she said **it is a book**, and shut it, and put the elastic band round it.

**And she went back onto the practice on 15 May, on her own, up the east side, and knocked at one door and said a man's name at it, and somebody answered, and she said nothing else, and nobody in that street asked her a single question about any of it and about four of them noticed.**

## Frank Dunning, about 65 — 88 Sillick Lane

**He has said *that is the correct arrangement and it is the worst arrangement in the world, in that order* four times — 28 January, 25 April, 13 May, 31 May — and has been right four times, and on 31 May it was the last thing said in that room.** He said the fourth rule *somebody is keeping it* three times in this batch and conceded to the woman of about fifty from the sheet that it was three and not two. **He puts the rota in the plastic wallet and then takes it out again and puts it back on top, and he ticks it in pencil in his own hand, and everybody who has ever held that wallet knows a pencil tick in a plastic wallet in a hall is not a record.** He is the one who says so, out loud, when about six people go out from three o'clock on a Sunday in May and get four doors done in half an hour in the dark: **the second rule is the light and not a clock, and they stop.** He is owed nothing and is on no list of people owed anything and nobody will ever knock at his door, and that is the practice working exactly as designed.

## The man of fifty-two — first-floor flat on the east side of Sillick Lane, twenty-six years, unnamed

**He is the volume's argument, and he said it in the same words three times in ten days, and nobody ever asks him.** *A list of houses is worse than a list of people*, in a room with a piece of A4 on it, with about eleven people, and it was the first thing anybody had given the street, and both halves are true and he has been trying since January to get rid of one of them and cannot. *You cannot put a duty in a piece of paper, and a piece of paper holds a name against a date and does not hold the bit in the middle, which is the part where the person is allowed to stop.* *A rota cannot hold a permission; you cannot scale a permission; a suggestion with a list of names on it is the worst kind of list there is, because it looks like somebody is in it.* *Signing is not agreeing.* *Whatever this is, it has stopped at that door; it has come up the stairs and it has not come in, and there is a date on the end of that paper and it did not come in on the other side of it, and I do not know that it knows about the paper and I am not going to stand in a room and tell anybody that it does.* **He has been waiting four months for somebody to make him say a thing and nobody makes him. He says it anyway. Every time.** He is fifty-two and he is not paid and nobody asked him, and on 20 May he said his no out loud in the same room on the same afternoon and did not soften it and then said both halves anyway, and asked for it to be written down that the man who said no to it is the man who has read it out twice.

## The woman of about fifty — of Sillick Lane, and the person who keeps the fifth line

**She has been in the practice since 8 January 2025 and has never been asked and has never been asked to explain anything she has done.** She is the person who has always taken the boundary down, has never once been asked to and has never once said she would. **She said the word *name* out loud at about ten to five on 20 May and a woman of fifty-four said no in about four seconds.** She is the one who said "I did not" when a room asked who else had heard a knock, **and that was the most useful thing anybody said in that room all day, because it meant there were two of them and not five, and she had made it two by answering.** On 30 May she put her hand flat on the table at the row for number thirty, said **that is me, that is my mother's years**, and then struck through 1982 herself, wrote *before 1982, to be confirmed* over it, stopped, and asked whether she might put *I do not know how far back* on it. **Her mother died in 1998 and had the years off a council letter about the rent, about the account being transferred, and never had that letter in front of her again.**

## Dorothy Marsden, 61 — NEW IN THIS BATCH, a member of the Lantern Society

**A first-floor suite on Lantern Row, instructed her solicitors in January 2025, and made nine acquisitions of household effects between 15 December 2024 and 9 March 2025 at addresses where the field had emptied.** Her screening schedule has five columns and **the fifth is headed TRACEABLE PRIOR OCCUPANT and is filled in once, in the fourth row, with a name, and beside the name the word ESTABLISHED, and underneath it in a different hand NOT PURSUED.** She screened and signed as a member; a second member countersigned with a four-digit number and no name. **She instructed her solicitor to say plainly, in nine sentences, that she acquires only at addresses at which no traceable prior occupant can be established, and does not pursue an address at which one can, and that she regards this as ordinary care for the vendor's privacy and not as anything else.** She has done nothing wrong. **The registrar wrote her name into a file on the fourth floor of a building on a hill, in a file about a request for a document and not about her, and said out loud that it caught her doing something lawful, that there is no forum in this city in which a person may say that a collector has decided to buy only where nobody can be found, and that nobody has refused one and none has ever been designed.** She is not a villain and Volume 03 must not make her one, and the Society's full escalation is Volume 10.

## Mavis Ludlow, 61 — of 22 Sillick Lane, NEW IN THIS BATCH

**Thirty-one years in that flat, and before it the one above it with her aunt, a cook at St Anselm's until 2019, and she has got the knee.** Read the boundary twice on Monday 10 May and would not let anybody read it out loud. **Asked what happens to the signature afterwards and was told in about nine seconds, without a pause and without any attempt to make it better than it was: it goes in that wallet on that table, and in about four years it will be in a file in a room in a building, and it will not be in this hall, and nobody will be able to take it back, and your name will be on it and the address and the date and a thing you did on a Saturday afternoon, and I cannot tell you which building and I would not tell you if I could, because if you knew the building you would start being careful about this and the whole point of it is that you are not careful about it.** She called that the worst thing anybody had said to her in about two years, and signed anyway, and gave the reason unasked: **in December a letter came to number twenty-two addressed to the house; it knows where she lives and it does not know who she is; and she has lived there for thirty-one years and there is not one piece of paper in this city with her name on it and that address on it, and she is owed a paper. That is all of it.** She knows what a name in a field is, because she has been in a queue in a library back room for six years watching what happens to people whose names go into fields. **She is the first signature in this case in twenty-two weeks, and she said her own name out loud at three of four doors on 15 May, which she had not done the week before, and somebody heard her say it and did not comment.**

## The woman of forty-four — first-floor flat on the east side of Sillick Lane

**Nineteen years of rent, every month, no gap, into a counter in a book in an office in Pemberton Row, and she wants the money, and she is owed it by nobody, and she says so herself before anybody says it.** On 27 May, standing up, as she always does: **I have been owed money by nobody since 2006 and this does not change that and it does not pay for it. If anybody in this corridor thinks four flats and a weekly rent answers nineteen years of rent, they have misunderstood me, and I would rather say that now than in November.** And the one thing the arrangement does: **it means seven of us are not scattered across four miles in October, and that is all it does, and all it does is worth having, and I am not going to be nicer about it than that because I have been honest with the nine of you since April and I am not going to start.** ⚠ **Twenty-two is the housing officer's years of service. Nineteen is the tenant's years of rent, and it is the figure in Chapter 86, Chapter 90, her own mouth in Chapter 96 and every state file, and a later pass must not let the two migrate again.**

## Mrs Toft, about fifty — records officer, district addressing service, Marsh Lane

**Came forty miles on her own time, said the true sentence in about nine seconds, and then said what it costs, which is her post.** A list of about nine addresses and the years is not a list of people; it is a list of houses; **that is the whole of the difference and the only difference and it is the first thing this case has ever been able to offer a street.** Then: **it costs somebody their post, and the somebody is me**, because the column is the district's own retained value of a field it has already overwritten, carried on the front sheet of every morning's supplied-file archive, a binder of every morning from 1 November to 19 January and after that date nothing. **She offered it anyway, so that it is on the table that it exists, and was refused twice, and asked that it be recorded that she offered it and that it was refused, that she is not offended, and that she would make the same offer in four years to somebody else if she is still in post, because the day somebody builds a form for this is the day it should be asked for and not before.** Clause six bit the person who most wanted to be helpful and she brought it to the table herself. She has been asked once in six years and both times the request came from this street.

## Sonia Boulton, about thirty-nine — letting agent, MRICS, 11 Pemberton Row

**She has refused the book seven times in this case and eight in this batch, kindly, and the book is still shut, and it is still shut after the Christmas cards are found in nine drawers in front of her.** She has said the one checkable thing anybody said in the batch: **for any household in blocks one to six the firm will confirm in writing the date its notice was served and the date of the term end, on production of the notice, or any part of it, or a description of the notice; it is free; it takes four days; it has been available since 11 February and her own firm has never mentioned it to a tenant; about four of the nine addresses are in blocks one to six.** The other five are not in a block, or the block has gone, or there was no notice, and **she is not going to invent a date to fill a gap in that street.** And the sentence nobody is going to like: **a notice is a document that says a landlord has decided something about a house. It is the one piece of paper any of you have that says you were there, and it went to the house, and the house stopped being anybody's, and the notice went with it. That is not a system failure. That is a letter in a box.** ⚠ **And she was told about the cards in about eleven seconds and conceded one half of what she had said in four sentences and held the other half — a card in a tin is not a notice, does not stop a tenancy, and will not stop anything in October either — and that is the record, because a room whose whole method is correcting each other is not allowed to let a competent person be wrong in a room without noticing.**

## The housing officer, about fifty-eight — NEW IN THIS BATCH

**A duty, nine separate asks in a corridor on the first floor at Cauldwell Buildings, in about four minutes each, the same way nine times, and she wrote down what she was asked to write down and asked first whether she might.** Seven yes, two no, **and both of the two said yes to the writing**, and one of the two said no with a four-sentence reason — nineteen years of rent, a weekly rent can be stopped in a week and a monthly one cannot, and she would rather be somewhere she can be turned out of in a month than somewhere she can be turned out of on a Friday — **and the other gave no reason at all and the officer wrote DECLINED — NO REASON GIVEN and read it back and the woman said it was right.** A registrar of twenty-eight stood in that corridor and said that was the first refusal in this case ever to be written down as a refusal, **and that it is not a victory and she wanted it said before somebody in that corridor started calling it one.** She is the only person in the batch who asked Elias Venn a question and took a no for the reason he gave.

## The woman of about thirty with the ring of keys — NEW IN THIS BATCH, Sillick Lane branch library

**About thirty, a cardigan with a pen in the pocket, Mr Perrin has the keys and finishes at half past five and has never once had to wait in a doorway.** She read the booking form back before she took the money, **because the form has one box and the box is for the day it is used, so she wrote the date of the booking out herself as well — and that form is the plainest object in the case and it is in a drawer in a library and nobody will ever see it.** She told them to take all of the library's tape, because the tape and the pins are the library's and a pin left in that wall at half past five becomes a number and a job for somebody in a building on a hill. **She said *that'll come off* and it did, in about nine seconds, with a bit of elbow grease, and she keeps the bit in a drawer and has had to twice in about two years.** And at about five to six, with the tape in her hand, she asked the object-or-place question in eleven words because she has to wind the alarm off at six. **She has not given her name and nobody has asked her for it.**

## Mairin Devlin, 31 — registration officer

**She tried to keep the fourth rule on 3 May and could not, and the reason she gave is the best one anybody in this case has given for not writing something down.** Her office holds a record. A record of who is on a street is a list. **Twenty-five years of who is on a street, with a person's name against every line, retrievable by anybody in the council who has a reason and by anybody else who can find a reason, and about four hundred people, and that is not a guess, and she has not counted and is not going to count, because she has spent nine days working out whether there is one version of this that is not a list and there is not, and she has checked in the only place she can check, which is her own form, and the form does not care what I intend it for.** **It is that there is nowhere for it to go that is not a list. That is a worse sentence than yours and it took me nine days and I have had a form the whole time.** She will write to her office on Tuesday: a form is not available for the fourth rule, and the letter will not say why, and the reason will be in a minute in her own office that will not leave her office. **And she refused to hand a form to a museum and asked nobody else to, and said she was not going to be the one who asks** — a record of that street in that building, in a file, is a list of people in a building that keeps records, and in four years there is a person with a job who has never met any of us whose whole function is that file.

## The registrar, the requester, and the panel — NOT NAMED, AND NOT TO BE NAMED

**The panel on the case at plinth thirty-four was printed at a kitchen table in Low Ward by a woman of fifty-four and taped on on 27 November 2024, and its last line says *the pay office kept its own book. That book is on the table*.** It is a hundred and eighty-five days old at the end of this volume. **It was not lifted, the tape was not peeled, and the card underneath was not read by anybody, including by the registrar and including by the Director, who were standing six inches from it, and it is the most carefully kept document in that building.** The requester was asked about it in a public room on 21 January 2025 and declined in a public room on the same afternoon, and has not been asked since. **The correction of 25 February 2025 is under it and will be there for ever and nobody will ever read it by lifting the tape.**

## Relationships, end of Volume 02

- **Elias / Mara:** a working arrangement that has become **reliable, in the procedural form, and not in any other form.** She has put a false name into a public document and told him the cost of it in advance. He has found the error in his own instrument in a room full of the people whose facts it was. **She still has not read his notebook and has given the reason herself, and he has still not asked her to, and the fifth line is still not hers.** No romance. No confession. **The volume's last recorded exchange between them is about who checks a thing, and it is not about him.**
- **Elias / Ivo:** a working partnership in which Ivo stopped looking and said so in a room, and Elias found the one thing in nine pages that Ivo's finding depended on. **Ivo's condition from 1 December 2024, that every photographic exposure of collections material by a member of staff must be countersigned at the point of making, is in force and was not argued about in this batch.**
- **Elias / Anja:** suspicion, and the first instrument either of them has put in a stranger's hands on purpose. **She put a false name in a public document and refused to put a tidier one there, and she will not have a favour in a public document wearing a refusal's clothes. He is on a payroll at a cost centre she signed, and neither of them has said so.**
- **Elias / Hattie Bramm:** **he has still not said the moral floor and did not say it here, and she said it four times in ten days in rooms he was not in, and he wrote it down afterwards.** He has the sentence since December and has not given it away.
- **Elias / the residents of Sillick Lane:** **he has not been asked seven times in five weeks, and he has been given the one good instrument in the case by a woman of fifty-four who wants somebody who has been on the wrong end of a piece of paper to be the one holding it, and he has accepted it, and he is not going to hold it by himself.** A man of fifty-two has told him to do less and he obeyed. A man of seventy-four has read his page back to him and found the error.
- **Elias / Cliff Sowerby:** the only person in the volume who has ever picked up the end of something he wrote. **Nobody wrote it down.**
- **Elias / the Bell at 11:52:** untouched, unspoken, and not referred to. **The bell has now been silent for one hundred and sixty-nine days and no bell is on a step and nobody has ever asked the two of them about each other and nobody is going to.**

# CHARACTER STATE — THE VOLUME 02 CLOSE, and the Volume 03 inheritance

**Verified against the chapter files, not against this file, because this file has been wrong before and because the last review found the state layer certifying the prose instead of auditing it.**

## THE END OF VOLUME 02, IN ONE LINE EACH, FOR THE RELATIONSHIPS THAT MATTER

- **Elias / Mara — suspicion and professional competition, and the position is exactly where the series outline says it should be after V2.** *I do not know what you are for* is never unsaid. She refused to read a second notebook in the same case, in writing. She refused his offer of help on 27 May and he said no to a seat in a building with a landlord and was right. **There is no romance, no confession, no softening and no trust, and she is not in Chapter 100.** `outline/series.md` puts **first reliable trust in V3–V4**, and Volume 03 is the first movement of that, and it must be paid for: **she reads a notebook and the reading does not go well, and neither of them says the word trust, and no chapter may.**
- **Elias / Ivo — a working partnership in which the volume's last statement is an absence.** He built the clamps and the handrails and the cover and the fail-safe, he found the lawful door in about nine seconds in December, and he is the systems technician whose *about forty million to one, and the sum is remarkable, and I am not going to say the next sentence because I have not checked what it is* is the volume's central finding. **He is in zero words of nine of the last ten chapters and one line of Chapter 98.** **Volume 03 must put him back in Movement 1**, and his condition of 1 December 2024 — every photographic exposure of collections material by a member of staff countersigned at the point of making — is in force and bites.
- **Elias / Anja — suspicion with a duty, and her last appearance is eleven seconds of silence.** She said the 1902 sentence out loud in a public room and did not offer a writer. She refused a favour in a public document wearing a refusal's clothes. She says the Museum is the last institution in the chain and not the first. ⚠ **Corrected by this pass: she is not absent from the last ten chapters. She is in one line of Chapter 97** — *Anja Ward read the note twice at about half past eleven on the Wednesday and did not say anything for about nine seconds, which she does not do* — **and in none of the other nine.** **She is not an ally, she is not forgiven, and Volume 03 must put her back on the page without making her one.**
- **Elias / Hesta — NOT STARTED, AND IT IS THE VOLUME'S ONE MISSED RELATIONSHIP BEAT.** Zero chapters in two volumes. She is 61 and canon and she is in `bible/characters.md`. **The line opens in Volume 03, four volumes late, and the displacement is recorded in `outline/volume-03.md` as a hard lock rather than hidden.**
- **Elias / the residents of Sillick Lane — he has been asked seven times in five weeks and has been given the one good instrument in the case by a woman who is owed nothing.** They are not an ally and they are not a client and they did not ask him.

## THE RESIDENTS OF SILLICK LANE — THE BIBLE DECISION, AND IT IS NOT PROMOTION

**They are NOT promoted to `bible/characters.md` as individuals, and the reason is the volume's own argument: a list of houses is worse than a list of people, and a bible cast is a list of people.** What is promoted instead is the **group, once, as a named volume-local body with a written position, in `bible/world.md` under the heading *The Group That Is Not a Cast: the residents of Sillick Lane, and the four flats at Cauldwell Buildings***, so that a Volume 04 writer inherits the street without inheriting a roll of the people on it. ⚠ **The heading carries both addresses on purpose: the nine households are of Sillick Lane and their date is 1 October 2025, and the four flats, the seven households, the weekly rent and the term to 30 November 2025 are at Cauldwell Buildings, which is a different building in a different street, and Chapter 96 is the reason the two may never be merged into a single sentence.** **The one individual promoted is Vera Pilling**, on her own evidence and not on her usefulness: she is the register's keeper, and the reason she is the keeper is a qualification and not a skill — *I am eighty-one and I am not going to be here in four years, and I want that written down.* Everyone else stays here, which is the volume-local record, and is where Volume 01 kept Cliff Sowerby, Perry Loach, Wynne Loach, Doreen Sarn and Hattie Bramm. **Nobody is promoted because a later volume will want them.**

## VOLUME 03'S NEW PEOPLE. All competent. None a villain. None a fool. None a mask. None a network.

- **Marianne Cope**, about thirty-four, **a flood claims handler.** She has read page one of a two-page instruction about four hundred adjusters were given in about 2011 and she is the only person in this business who has. **She is right about the box on page one and she cannot do a single thing about it, and the chapter must let the reader see that coming and must not let her say it.** Not a villain, not a fraud, and she is not rescued by a man with a power.
- **Colin Bray**, about forty-four, **a salvage contractor.** Two crews, a docket book, and **a weight column that a form asks for and nobody can check, with the money attached to it.** He pays people on a Friday and is owed money himself and says both. **Nobody is a cartel here and he is not the face of one.**
- **Gwilym Petherick**, about fifty-five, **a river engineer.** A printed table of datums, a wall, and a rule. He is right that two numbers do not agree and he cannot say which is right. ⚠ **CORRECTED 2026-09-26: he is the instrument by which Volume 02's live question is answered and he is not the one who answers it.** The measurement is on the reach rope — **knots at intervals that are not at intervals, and not irregular either, because they are a tide-mark series put in by a person who knew the water** — and his printed table of datums is what turns that series into a series of heights. **So the answer arrives as a number beside a knot, in a corridor, and he never says the sentence *is the pattern the object or the place*, and nobody is permitted to make him say it.** This file previously said he *answers the question with a measurement instead of a sentence*, which is the same sentence wearing different clothes; the shape in `outline/volume-03.md` and `bible/characters.md` is now the rope's, and this file matches it.
- **Averil Shand**, about sixty-eight, **registrar of burials for a river parish, forty-one years.** A hardback of interments, and a facsimile of one page produced on the grounds that **a page is not a list.** She is the moral argument of Movement 2 given a person and a form.
- **Yvette Carrow**, about forty-eight, **the office that issues a flood-emergency access suspension.** A named officer, a numbered power, a form with a box for a period, and a suspension that is lawful, dated, signed and **correct about the water and catastrophic about the people.** **She is proved right in the middle of the volume. That is the point of her.**
- **Ada Iles**, **Mara's mother.** She committed a falsification that saved people and made later erasure harder to trace. **She is not a villain and Volume 03's resolution says so in a sentence.**
- **Dorothy Marsden, 61** — **unchanged, unresolved, and not to be resolved.** A member of the Lantern Society, named in a file about a document request and not about her, who instructed a rule in writing and **has done nothing wrong.** A second member countersigns with a four-digit number and no name. **The Society's full escalation is Volume 10.**

# CHARACTER STATE — VOLUME 03, BATCH 0001 (Chapters 101–110), as of Sunday 4 July 2025

**Verified against `chapters/volume-03/` and not against the rest of this state layer.** The residents of Sillick Lane stay a group and are not promoted, for the reason the manuscript's own argument gives. **Nobody in this batch is promoted to `bible/characters.md` on usefulness**, and the two who might be argued for — Marianne Cope and Gwilym Petherick — have earned entries on their own evidence and are recorded here first.

## THE RELATIONSHIPS, ONE LINE EACH, AND NONE OF THEM IS SETTLED

- **Elias / Mara — reliable, in the procedural form, and paid for.** She is on the page in four of the ten chapters and not in six, and that is a person with a job. **She has read page one of the two-page instruction, on the Wednesday night, in about an hour, and was not asked to.** She found two things: the drafting is good work and section B is the best sentence anybody in this case has read in twenty-six weeks, and there is nothing on either page that asks how many people were in the house. She has written to the issuing office for the consultation behind section B, has put no name of his anywhere on the letter, and **told him the answer takes four weeks so that he cannot go and count.** **She refused, out loud, to say what she thinks the box is for, and gave the reason: the person who owns that instruction is a woman on the second floor of a building on Wharf Street who has done nothing wrong, and a registrar from a hill has not got the standing.** On 28 June she chaired a meeting she has no authority to chair, said the cost out loud first, and **at about eight o'clock asked in a room the question she had refused him three times.** On 4 July she read all eleven sheets of somebody else's claim file aloud in a corridor to a woman who is owed a piece of paper and **told her the truth, which is that this flood produces one kind of paper and she is not in it.** **Neither of them has said the word trust and no chapter may.**
- **Elias / Ivo — the partnership, and the condition, and what the condition cost.** He is in four of the ten chapters and he is the reason two of the batch's best facts are on the page. **He came back because the drainage invert at the Museum's loading bay is about fourteen inches below the drawing, and he brought a camera to a wharf and did not use it**, and he said exactly why, in a street, in about nine seconds: a docket book is not collections material and he has known that since 2 December and would like somebody with a duty to say it in a room so that he can stop having the conversation with himself at half past three in the afternoon. He wrote the column out on the back of a docket, photographed **that**, countersigned his own initials with the time, and wrote underneath **COUNTERSIGNATURE UNAVAILABLE — ONE MEMBER OF STAFF PRESENT**, and said it would be a problem for somebody in about four years. **He made a joke, once, on a wet street, and it was not good and was not meant to be: three rules and a kerb, and I am the rule-holder.** In the lock yard he is the man the question is said to instead of the man it should have been said to, and Elias calls that cowardice on the page.
- **Elias / Anja — suspicion with a duty, and a Director who is right and not an ally.** Two appearances, both by telephone. **She says no, it is not collections material and it never has been and nothing in that building can make it collections material by photographing it. And she says take it.** And when the countersignature is the obstacle she does not wave it through: she will countersign four exposures on Monday morning, he may take them on Monday afternoon, and if it is needed before Monday she will come and stand on a wet wall herself. **That cost the case a clean tide line and she is right, and the man who wrote the condition is the one who was standing on the quay.** And on 28 June she rang a registrar at four o'clock to say she did not have to be there and did not take it back when she was told she would stay, and she said that a museum that holds nothing is going to be described in about four years as a museum that was careful. **She is not forgiven. She is not an ally. She said thank you and wrote something down and stopped, and it is about eleven weeks since anybody thanked her for a piece of paper she did not have.**
- **Elias / Hattie Bramm — still not asked.** Not in this batch, and the silence is now twenty-eight weeks and it is deliberate on her side and uncomfortable on his.
- **Elias / the residents of Sillick Lane — he was in a room with five of them for five and a half hours and wrote nothing down, and that is the relationship.** The man of fifty-two said the answer to the batch and did not know it and said so; Vera Pilling gave the same word to yes and to no; Frank Dunning declined to say his sentence a fifth time in a hall and offered the fourth instead. **Nobody asked Elias anything in five and a half hours and he had been asked nothing seven times in five weeks in May and had not known what to do with it, and the woman of about fifty said: then you are doing it right.**
- **Elias / Cliff Sowerby — nothing happened.** The fortnight he asked for runs out on about 13 June. **He has not rung him. That is the first thing in twenty-eight weeks he has simply not done, and it is not in the notebook, and it is the only outstanding thing he is aware of that he has chosen to leave.**

## MARIANNE COPE, about 34 — flood claims handler, City Flood Office, Wharf Street. NEW IN THIS BATCH.

**Nine years in the trade and she is the person who sends the instruction wallets out.** She has read page one and she keeps the current print and the archive of the previous four, and the original had been folded in half four times in a decade and gone soft at the creases, which is why there is a clean copy. **Four hundred and eleven adjusters, contractors and loss adjusters' clerks have been issued it between 2011 and November 2024. She has asked four people whether they read page one, knows the answer for two, has stopped asking, and has started a pencil note in the back of her own copy — thirty-one lines, which adjuster read which box out of the form to her on the telephone. It is not a record. It is in pencil in the back of a plastic wallet and if she leaves this firm it goes in a bin.**

**She is the person who will be proved right about the box in Chapter 111 and who will not be able to do one single thing about it, and the chapter must let the reader see that coming and must not let her say it.** She has said, out loud and in advance, that a man in a coat can look at nothing in her office and why, and that she would answer what she is allowed to answer and say which parts those are, and that he would then be disappointed and not be able to do one thing about it. **She read Part 5 sections A to D out of a clean copy in a chair and then would not say it in her own words, and gave the reason, and the reason is that she has not got the standing and neither has he.** She asked two people who used to be senior to her what the box is for; both are gone; one said the audit trail and one said the statistics; **and she has been doing this for nine years and she is telling him they were guessing, and they were guessing in a room where she was junior, which is how you guess.**
